Affine Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials on the subregular cell in non simply-laced Lie algebras with an application to character formulae
Vasily Krylov, Kenta Suzuki

Abstract
. We extend the techniques in [ BKK24 ] to the non-simply-laced situation, and calculate explicit special values of parabolic affine inverse Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials for subregular nilpotent orbits. We thus obtain explicit character formulas for certain irreducible representations of affine Lie algebras. As particular cases, we compute characters of simple vertex algebras V k ​ ( 𝔤 ) V_{k}(\mathfrak{g}) for k = − 1 , … , − b k=-1,\ldots,-b , where b b is the largest label of the highest short coroot θ ∨ \theta^{\vee} . Conjecturally, all ordinary modules over V − b ​ ( 𝔤 ) V_{-b}(\mathfrak{g}) are covered by our computations. As an application, we obtain the explicit formulas for flavoured Schur indices of rank one Argyres–Douglas 4d SCFTs with flavour symmetry G 2 G_{2} and B 3 B_{3} . Our results are proved using the geometry of the Springer resolution. We identify the cell quotient of the anti-spherical module over W ^ \widehat{W} corresponding to the subregular cell with a certain one-dimensional extension of a module defined by Lusztig in [ Lus83 ] . We describe the canonical basis in this module geometrically and present an explicit description of the corresponding objects in the derived category of equivariant coherent sheaves on the Springer resolution. They correspond to irreducible objects in the heart of a certain t t -structure that we describe using an equivariant version of the derived McKay correspondence.



1.
Introduction

Let
G
be a simple, simply-connected reductive group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, and let
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
be the associated affine Lie algebra. Let
\widehat{W}=Q^{\vee}\rtimes W
be the affine Weyl group, which acts (denoted by
\circ
) on the dual of the Cartan
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}
by the shifted action by the sum of the fundamental weights
\widehat{\rho}
. For each character
\Lambda\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}
, we may define two
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules:
M(\Lambda)
, the Verma module, and
L(\Lambda)
, the unique irreducible quotient of
M(\Lambda)
(see §
2.9
for details). Although by construction the character of
M(\Lambda)
is easy to describe, the character of
L(\Lambda)
is much more complicated to describe. For
\kappa>-h^{\vee}
where
h^{\vee}
is the dual Coxeter number, let
{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}
be the category
{\mathcal{O}}
of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules of level
\kappa
with some finiteness conditions, as in
[
KT90
]
(see Definition
2.31
). For
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}
regular (i.e., the stabilizer of the
\widehat{W}
-action is trivial) with integer level
\kappa=\kappa(\lambda)>-h^{\vee}
and
v\in\widehat{W}
, there exists an equality in the
K
-theory of the category
{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}

[
KT00
, Thm 1.1]
:

[L(v^{-1}\circ\lambda)]=\sum_{w\in\widehat{W}}\epsilon(wv^{-1})\mathbf{m}_{v}^{w}[M(w^{-1}\circ\lambda)],

where
\epsilon\colon\widehat{W}\rightarrow\{\pm 1\}
is the sign, and
\mathbf{m}_{v}^{w}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbf{m}_{v}^{w}(1)
are special values of the inverse parabolic Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials. Thus, calculating the character of
L(\Lambda)
is essentially equivalent to calculating
\mathbf{m}_{v}^{w}
. As in
[
BKK24
, Definition 3.7]
, we define:

Definition 1.1
.

Let
\widehat{W}^{f}
be the set of
v\in\widehat{W}
such that
v
is the shortest element in
vW
. There is a bijection
\widehat{W}^{f}\rightarrow\widehat{W}/W\simeq Q^{\vee}
, and we let
\nu\mapstochar\rightarrow w_{\nu}
be the inverse bijection.

In
[
KL79
]
, Kazhdan and Lusztig introduced a notion of a two-sided cell in
\widehat{W}
; Lusztig then proved in
[
Lus89
]
that there exists the bijection between two-sided cells in
\widehat{W}
and the set of nilpotent
G^{\vee}
-orbits.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
c_{\mathrm{subreg}}\subset\widehat{W}
be the two-sided cell, which corresponds to the subregular orbit. Let
c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\widehat{W}^{f}\cap c_{\mathrm{subreg}}
, which is a left cell in
\widehat{W}
.

1.1.
Main results of the paper

We calculate
\mathbf{m}_{v}^{w}
when
\mathfrak{g}
is not simply-laced and
v\in c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
, where
c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}\subset\widehat{W}
is the left cell defined above (the case of simply-laced
\mathfrak{g}
was treated in
[
BKK24
]
). This allows us to prove the following analog of
[
BKK24
, Thm 2.9]
:

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
B_{n}
(
n\geqslant 3
),
C_{n}
(
n\geqslant 2
), or
F_{4}
,
G_{2}
. Let
w\in c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
and let
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}
be such that
\Lambda=w^{-1}(\lambda)
is quasi-dominant, where
w
is the longest element in the coset
\widehat{W}_{\lambda}w
. Then

\widehat{R}\operatorname{ch}L(\Lambda)=\sum_{u\in W}\epsilon(uw)\sum_{\gamma\in Q^{\vee}}\mathbf{m}^{w_{\gamma}}_{w}e^{ut_{\gamma}w(\Lambda+\widehat{\rho})},

where
m^{w_{\gamma}}_{w}
is given in Theorems
7.4
,
7.7
,
7.13
, and
7.19
.

The following Lemma describes all possible
\Lambda
’s that can appear in Theorem
1.3
and is the direct generalization of
[
BKK24
, Lemma 2.11 and Lemma 2.17]
. Recall that
c_{\mathrm{subreg}}\subset\widehat{W}
consists of the elements
w\in\widehat{W}\setminus\{1\}
that have
unique
minimal decomposition. For such an element
w
, we denote by
\mu(w)\in\widehat{S}
the first term of the minimal decomposition of
w
.

Lemma 1.4
.

Elements
\lambda\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}
,
w\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}
, and
\Lambda=w^{-1}\circ\lambda
satisfying the conditions in Theorem
1.3
are one of the following:

(1)

\lambda+\widehat{\rho}
is regular dominant integral and
w
is an arbitrary element of
c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
; or

(2)

\lambda=-\Lambda_{i}+\sum_{k\neq i}m_{k}\Lambda_{k}+x\delta
for
i=\mu(w)
,
x\in\mathbb{C}
, and
m_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
.

Proof.

Same as the proof of
[
BKK24
, Lemma 2.11]
.
∎

Remark 1.5
.

Note that the level
k:=\Lambda(K)
of
L(\Lambda)
appearing in Lemma
1.4
is
\geqslant-b
, where
b
is the maximal among the coefficients
a_{i}
in
\theta^{\vee}=\sum_{i}a_{i}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
. Furthermore,
\Lambda=k\Lambda_{0}
always satisfies the conditions of Lemma
1.4
for
k=-1,\ldots,-b
.

Remark 1.6
.

For example,
\Lambda=-\Lambda_{0}
always satisfies the conditions of Lemma
1.4
(for
\lambda=-\Lambda_{0}
, and
w=s_{0}\in c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
). Theorem
1.3
then provides an explicit formula for the character of
L(-\Lambda_{0})
. To illustrate, for
\mathfrak{g}=G_{2}
we get (see Example
8.3
):

(1)

\widehat{R}\operatorname{ch}L(-\Lambda_{0})=-\sum_{\gamma=m\alpha^{\vee}+n\beta^{\vee}}\sum_{u\in W}\epsilon(u)\Big(\Big\lfloor\frac{m-1}{3}\Big\rfloor+\frac{1}{4}\!(m-\mathbf{1}_{m\equiv n\bmod 2}-\mathbf{1}_{n\equiv 0\bmod 2})\Big)e^{ut_{-\gamma}(-\Lambda_{0}+\widehat{\rho})},

where
\alpha^{\vee}
,
\beta^{\vee}
are the simple coroots of
\mathfrak{g}
(see Example
2.15
).

Remark 1.7
.

When
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
G_{2}
or
B_{3}
,
\Lambda=-2\Lambda_{0}
also satisfies the conditions of Lemma
1.4
. When
\mathfrak{g}=G_{2}
,

(2)

\widehat{R}\operatorname{ch}L(-2\Lambda_{0})=-\sum_{\gamma=m\alpha^{\vee}+n\beta^{\vee}}\sum_{u\in W}\epsilon(u)\Big(\Big\lfloor\frac{m-1}{3}\Big\rfloor+\frac{1}{4}\!(m-\mathbf{1}_{m\equiv n\bmod 2}-\mathbf{1}_{n\equiv 0\bmod 2})\Big)e^{ut_{-\gamma}(-2\Lambda_{0}+\widehat{\rho})}.

When
\mathfrak{g}
is of type
B_{3}
, we have (see Example
8.4
)

(3)

\widehat{R}\operatorname{ch}L(-2\Lambda_{0})=-\sum_{\gamma=a_{1}\alpha_{1}^{\vee}+a_{2}\alpha_{2}^{\vee}+b\beta^{\vee}}\sum_{u\in W}\epsilon(u)\bigg(\frac{1}{4}(a_{1}-1)-\frac{1}{4}(-1)^{a_{1}}\mathbf{1}_{a_{2}\not\equiv 0\bmod 2}\bigg)e^{ut_{-\gamma}(-2\Lambda_{0}+\widehat{\rho})},

where
\alpha^{\vee}
,
\beta^{\vee}
are the simple corrots of
\mathfrak{g}
(see Example
2.12
).

To calculate, we use the fact that the
\mathbf{m}_{v}^{w}
are realized in the
Q^{\vee}\subset\widehat{W}
-action on
K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}})
, where
\pi\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{N}}
is the Springer resolution of the nilpotent cone in
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
; explicitly,
K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}})
has the standard basis
\{[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}(\gamma)]:\gamma\in Q^{\vee}\}
and the canonical basis, i.e., the irreducible objects of the heart of the exotic
t
-structure on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}))
, see
[
Bez06
]
and
[
Bez16
]
. Moreover, letting
U\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}\cup{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{reg}}\subset{\mathcal{N}}
be an open subset consisting of the regular and subregular nilpotent orbits, on which we have the restricted Springer resolution
\widetilde{U}\rightarrow U
, there is a surjection
K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}})\twoheadrightarrow K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U})
. Moreover,
K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U})
has a canonical basis
\{\overline{C}_{w}:w\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}\cup\{1\}\}
consisting of classes of the irreducible objects of the heart of the exotic
t
-structure on
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U}))
(see Section
4
). In Appendix §
A
we
explicitly characterize the irreducible objects by
proving an equivariant version of the derived McKay correspondence, and observing that the standard
t
-structure on the derived category of double quiver representations transfers to the exotic
t
-structure.
We give an explicit description of these irreducible exotic coherent sheaves in Section
4
:

Theorem 1.8
.

The irreducible exotic coherent sheaves of
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U}))
is described in Propositions
4.9
,
4.18
,
4.21
, and
4.24
.

When
w=s_{0}
, we can go further, and describe the irreducible object corresponding to
s_{0}
in
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}))
, not just its restriction to
\widetilde{U}
:

Proposition 1.9

(Proposition
2.27
)
.

{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\overline{{\mathbb{O}}}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}[-1]\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}))
is an irreducible exotic coherent sheaf corresponding to
s_{0}
.

Now,
c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}
is a left cell, so by
[
Lus83
, Proposition 3.8]
there is a
\widehat{W}
-module
E_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}
with basis
(e_{w})_{w\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}}
.
In §
3
we define an extension
\widetilde{E}_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}=E_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}\oplus\mathbb{Z}e_{1}
of
E_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}
, which fits into an exact sequence

0\rightarrow E_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}\rightarrow\widetilde{E}_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow 0,

where
{\mathbb{Z}}
is the trivial representation of
\widehat{W}
. Let
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{sgn}}
be the sign representation of
\widehat{W}
.

Proposition 1.10
.

There is an isomorphism of exact sequences of
\widehat{W}
-modules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{sgn}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{E}^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{sgn}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{sgn}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{reg}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the middle isomorphism sends
e_{w}
to
C_{w}
for
w\in c_{\mathrm{reg}}\cup c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
.

Remark 1.11
.

In fact, all of the modules in Proposition
1.10
can be extended to
\mathcal{H}_{q}(\widehat{W})
-modules (see also Proposition
3.4
), and the isomorphisms extend.

Remark 1.12
.

In §
6
, we use Proposition
1.10
to prove a conjecture of
[
BKK23
]
and provide a description of
K^{G^{\vee}}(\widetilde{U})
using the Cartan of Kac-Moody algebras.

Thus, calculating
\mathbf{m}_{w}^{w_{\gamma}}
for such elements reduces to calculating the class of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}(\gamma)
in
K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U})
, as an explicit linear combination of the canonical basis.

1.1.1.
Main result of the Appendix
B

Let us finish this section by stating the last main result of this text obtained in Appendix
B
written jointly with Roman Bezrukavnikov.

Definition 1.13
.

An element
e\in\mathcal{N}
is called
distinguished
if
it is not contained in a proper Levi subalgebra.
An element
e\in\mathcal{N}
is called
very distinguished
if every element
e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
such that
e\in\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{e^{\prime}}

is distinguished.

For example, subregular nilpotent
e\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
is distinguished iff
\mathfrak{g}
is not of types
A_{n}
,
C_{n}
. In all of these cases, using the explicit formulas for
{\bf{m}}^{w_{\gamma}}_{w}
,
w\in c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
obtained in the present paper (and in
[
BKK24
]
) we see that the function

\gamma\mapstochar\rightarrow{\bf{m}}^{w_{\gamma}}_{w}

is the sum of
quasi-polynomials
of degree
2\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{B}_{e}=2
and with periods equal to
orders
of the elements in the finite group
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
. In Appendix
B
we generalize this result to the case of an
arbitrary
very distinguished nilpotent element.

Let
e\in\mathcal{N}
be a very distinguished nilpotent element.
Set
U:=\bigsqcup_{e\in\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{e^{\prime}}}\mathbb{O}_{e^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{N}
. This is an open
G^{\vee}
-invariant subset of
\mathcal{N}
. Note that
K^{G^{\vee}}(\widetilde{U})
is finite dimensional (use that
e
is very distinguished).
Pick
\nu\in Q^{\vee}
such that
w_{\nu}
lies in the two-sided cell. Using the localization theorem in equivariant
K
-theory obtained by Edidin and Graham in
[
EG08
]
, we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 1.14

(see Theorem
B.8
)
.

The function
\gamma\mapstochar\rightarrow{\bf{m}}_{w_{\nu}}^{w_{\gamma}}
is a quasi-polynomial, and we can estimate its
period and degree.

1.2.
Applications to representations of vertex algebras and Schur indices of 4D SCFT’s

For
k\in\mathbb{C}
consider the vacuum
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
-module
V^{k}(\mathfrak{g})
of level
k
. It is known that
V^{k}(\mathfrak{g})
has a natural vertex algebra structure. Let
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
be the unique nonzero irreducible quotient of
V^{k}(\mathfrak{g})
. It is known that as
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
-module, we have
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})\simeq L(k\Lambda_{0})
. It makes sense to talk about the category
\mathcal{O}
for
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
. We propose the following conjecture, joint with Victor Kac. Recall that
b
is the maximal among the coefficients
a_{i}
in
\theta^{\vee}=\sum_{i}a_{i}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
.

Conjecture 1.15
.

Irreducible objects
L(\Lambda)\in\mathcal{O}(V_{-b}(\mathfrak{g}))
with integral
\Lambda
are in bijection with the set
\{w\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}\,|\,\mu(w)=s_{i}\,\text{s.t.}\,\Lambda_{i}(K)=b\}
. The bijection is explicitly given by

w\mapstochar\rightarrow\Lambda=w^{-1}\circ(-\Lambda_{i}).

In particular, for
\mathfrak{g}
s.t.
Z_{e}
is finite, the irreducible objects as above are in bijection with a certain
right
Lusztig cell in the affine Weyl group.

We have checked that Conjecture
1.15
is correct in all cases when the set
\operatorname{Irr}\mathcal{O}(V_{-b}(\mathfrak{g}))
is described explicitly (see
[
AP08
]
for type
A
,
[
AM18
]
for types
A_{2},D_{4},E_{6},E_{7},E_{8}
and
[
ADF+24
]
for types
B_{3}
,
G_{2}
). As was observed in
[
LY23
]
, vertex algebras
V_{-2}(\mathfrak{g})
for
\mathfrak{g}
of types
B_{3}
and
G_{2}
correspond to certain rank one Argyres–Douglas theories in four dimension with flavour symmetries
B_{3}
and
G_{2}
respectively. So, our formulas (
2
) and (
3
) provide an explicit answer for the flavored Schur indices of these theories.

Remark 1.16
.

In Conjecture
1.15
above we only consider simple vertex algebra
V_{k}(\mathfrak{g})
for
k=-b
. One can consider arbitrary
k\in\{-1,\ldots,-b\}
. We do not know if it is natural to expect that every integral
\Lambda
such that
L(\Lambda)\in\mathcal{O}(V_{k}(\mathfrak{g}))
is as in Lemma
1.4
above.

It is expected that the condition that
Z_{e}
is finite, corresponds to
V_{-b}(\mathfrak{g})
being
quasi-lisse
(see
[
AK18
]
), i.e., the associated variety
\operatorname{Ass}(V_{-b}(\mathfrak{g}))
should be contained in the nilpotent cone of
\mathfrak{g}
.

From now on, assume that
Z_{e}
is finite. The following conjecture was communicated to us by Peng Shan.

Conjecture 1.17
.

Let
D\colon\{\mathcal{N}_{\mathfrak{g}}/G\}\rightarrow\{\mathcal{N}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}/G^{\vee}\}
be the Barbasch-Vogan-Lusztig-Spaltenstein duality.
Then

\operatorname{Ass}(V_{-b}(\mathfrak{g}))=\overline{D(\mathbb{O}_{\mathrm{subreg}})}.

It would be very interesting to figure out if there is any precise relation between the two conjectures above. Note that the subregular
right
cell appears in Conjecture
1.15
and parametrizes irreducible objects of the corresponding category
\mathcal{O}
, while the subregular nilpotent orbit appears in Conjecture
1.17
and controls the associated variety of the corresponding vertex algebra; this should not be a coincidence. A similar relation is known to be true for
W
(instead of
\widehat{W}
), namely, it is known (see
[
KL79
, Remark 1.6]
) that the irreducible objects in the regular block of the category
\mathcal{O}
for
\mathfrak{g}
have the same annihilator iff their labels in
W
lie in the same
left
Lusztig cell (the replacement of right cell by left cell might have to do with the appearance of the BVLS duality in Conjecture
1.17
).
Finally, let us mention that the Conjecture
1.17
is known to be true in some cases (see
[
AM18
]
,
[
ADF+24
]
).

1.2.1.
Modular invariance vs quasi-polynomials

Recall that in Appendix
B
written jointly with Roman Bezrukavnikov, we prove that if
w_{\nu}\in\widehat{W}
lies in the two-sided cell corresponding to an arbitrary
very distinguished
nilpotent element, then the coefficients
m^{w_{\gamma}}_{w_{\nu}}
are
quasi-polynomials
in
\gamma
and we can estimate their
period and degree. So, the (normalized) characters of the corresponding irreducible
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules might have nice modular invariance properties in the sense that they satisfy certain modular linear differential equations (cf.
[
AK18
]
and references therein).
It would be interesting to study them in some examples. For example, for
\mathfrak{g}=G_{2}
, we obtain explicit formulas for the characters of
L(-\Lambda_{0})
,
L(-2\Lambda_{0})
(see (
1
) and (
2
)) and it would be very interesting to understand their modular invariance properties.
Note, for example, that the explicit character formulas obtained in
[
BKK24
]
were applied in
[
KW24
]
to obtain modular invariance of (normalized) characters of certain
W
-algebras.

1.3.
Structure of the paper

In §
2
, we cover preliminaries, on canonical bases, the Springer resolution, Slodowy varieties, and representations of affine Lie algebras. In §
2.7
, we use the geometry of the subregular Slodowy variety to deduce a description of the irreducible exotic coherent sheaf corresponding to
s_{0}
. In §
3
we review the definition of the subregular two-sided cell, and give and review the algebraic description of the subregular affine Hecke algebra given by
[
Xu19
]
. In §
4
we explicitly characterize the irreducible exotic coherent sheaves in
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}(\widetilde{U}))
for
G
non-simply-laced. In §
5
we use the explicit description of the irreducible objects to give a geometric proof of Proposition
1.10
, which describes
K^{G^{\vee}}(\widetilde{U})
combinatorially. In §
6
we re-phrase the combinatorial description of
K^{G^{\vee}}(\widetilde{U})
using the Cartan of Kac-Moody algebras. In §
7
we use our geometric description to compute Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials on
c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
. Finaly in §
8
we apply our results to compute new character formulas for representations of affine Lie algebras. In Appendix
A
we re-cast our description of the irreducible exotic sheaves using an equivariant version of the derieved McKay correspondence. In Appendix
B
, written jointly with Roman Bezrukavnikov, we show a general result that, in particular, explains why the Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials we compute in types B, D, E, F, and G are quasi-polynomial.

1.4.

This project is supported by the MIT UROP+ program. The authors thank Roman Bezrukavnikov for numerous helpful suggestions. The fisrt author is also grateful to Ivan Karpov for the useful discussions of the material of the Appendix
B
. Many thanks are also to Victor Kac, George Lusztig and Peng Shan.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notation

We fix the following notation:

•

G
is a simple simply-connected reductive group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Let

G^{\vee}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
be the dual Lie group and Lie algebra, respectively. We fix a pinning:
T\subset B_{0}\subset G
and
T^{\vee}\subset B_{0}^{\vee}\subset G^{\vee}
. Let
Q^{\vee}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hom(T^{\vee},\mathbb{G}_{m})=\hom(\mathbb{G}_{m},T)
be the root lattice of
G^{\vee}
, and let
Q
be the dual lattice. Any
\gamma\in Q^{\vee}
viewed as an element of
\widehat{W}
is denoted as
t_{\gamma}
. Let
\Phi\subset Q
be the root system for
G
, with simple roots
\Delta
, and let
\Phi^{\vee}
be the root system for
G^{\vee}
, with simple roots
\Delta^{\vee}
. There is a canonical bijection
\alpha\in\Delta\mapstochar\rightarrow\alpha^{\vee}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\big(\frac{2\alpha}{(\alpha,\alpha)},-\big)\in\Delta^{\vee}
.

•

Let
W
be the Weyl group of
G
and
G^{\vee}
(identified by
s_{\alpha}\mapstochar\rightarrow s_{\alpha^{\vee}}
), and let
\widehat{W}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Q^{\vee}\rtimes W
be the affine Weyl group of
G
. Let
\prec
denote the Bruhat order on
\widehat{W}
. Let
S
be the set of simple reflections of
W
, and let
\widehat{S}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}S\cup\{s_{0}\}
be the set of simple reflections of
\widehat{W}
. For
t_{1},t_{2}\in\widehat{S}
, let
m_{t_{1},t_{2}}
denote the order of
t_{1}t_{2}
.

•

For each
\alpha\in\Delta
let
P_{\alpha,0}^{\vee}\subset G^{\vee}
be the parabolic subgroup generated by
s_{\alpha}
and
B_{0}^{\vee}
(and denote the Lie algebra by
\mathfrak{p}_{\alpha,0}^{\vee}
). This provides a bijection between
\Delta
and conjugacy classes of sub-minimal parabolic subgroups of
G^{\vee}
.

•

{\mathscr{B}}={\mathscr{B}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}\simeq G^{\vee}/B_{0}^{\vee}
, the
flag variety
, is the set of Borel subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
. For each
\alpha\in\Delta
,
{\mathscr{P}}_{\alpha}\simeq G^{\vee}/P_{\alpha,0}^{\vee}
is the
partial flag variety
, the set of parabolic subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
conjugate to
\mathfrak{p}_{\alpha}^{\vee}
. There is a canonical projection
\pi_{i}\colon{\mathscr{B}}\rightarrow{\mathscr{P}}_{i}
, which sends
\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}
to the unique parabolic algebra
\mathfrak{p}_{\alpha}^{\vee}\supset\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}
conjugate to
\mathfrak{p}_{\alpha,0}^{\vee}
(equivalently, the projection
G^{\vee}/B^{\vee}_{0}\rightarrow G^{\vee}/P_{\alpha,0}^{\vee}
). The fibers of
\pi_{i}
are isomorphic to
P_{\alpha,0}^{\vee}/B_{0}^{\vee}\simeq\mathbf{P}^{1}
.

•

{\mathcal{N}}={\mathcal{N}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}
is the variety of nilpotent elements in
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
.

•

{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{reg}}
is the set of regular nilpotent elements in
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
, and
{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
is the set of subregular nilpotent elements in
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
. Let
U\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}\cup{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{reg}}
, an open and dense subset of
{\mathcal{N}}
.

We also use the following notation for representations of
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
, used to explicitly construct sub-regular nilpotent orbits in classical types:

•

For an integer
N\geqslant 0
, let
V(N)
be the unique irreducible
(N+1)
-dimensional representation of
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
.

•

Let
V(N)_{k}
be the
k
-th weight space of
V(N)
, so
V(N)=V(N)_{-N}\oplus V(N)_{2-N}\cdots V(N)_{N-2}\oplus V(N)_{N}
.

•

For
N
odd, let
\omega\colon V(N)\times V(N)\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
be the non-degenerate,
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
-equivariant symplectic form, which is unique up to scalar. For
N
even, similarly let
(-,-)
be the non-degenerate,
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
-equivariant symmetric form, which is unique up to scalar.

We will furthermore use the following notation for the subregular two-sided cell, see §
3
:

•

c_{\mathrm{subreg}}\subset\widehat{W}
is the subregular two-sided cell and
c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}\subset c_{\mathrm{subreg}}
is a left cell, as in Definition
1.2
.

•

\mu\colon c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}\rightarrow\widehat{S}
sends
w\in c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
to the unique
i\in\widehat{S}
such that
\ell(s_{i}w)<\ell(w)
.

Let
\pi\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}=T^{*}{\mathscr{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{N}}
be the
Springer resolution
:
T^{*}{\mathscr{B}}\simeq\{(e,\mathfrak{b}^{\vee})\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\times{\mathscr{B}}:e\in[\mathfrak{b}^{\vee},\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}]\}
, and
\pi(e,\mathfrak{b}^{\vee})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}e
. For each
e\in{\mathcal{N}}
, let
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\mathfrak{b}\in{\mathscr{B}}:e\in[\mathfrak{b}^{\vee},\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}]\}
be the scheme-theoretic fiber of
\pi
over
e
, called a
Springer fiber
. Given any locally closed subset
X\subset{\mathcal{N}}
, we denote the scheme-theoretic pre-image
\pi^{-1}(X)
as
\widetilde{X}
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{q}(\widehat{W})
be the Hecke algebra, with a free
{\mathbb{Z}}[q^{\pm 1}]
-basis
\{H_{w}\}_{w\in\widehat{W}}
. Then
[
KL79
]
defines
C_{\nu}\in\mathcal{H}_{q}(\widehat{W})
such that
\overline{C}_{v}=q^{-\ell(v)}C_{v}
and which admit an expansion

C_{\nu}=\sum_{w\preccurlyeq v}P_{w,v}(q)H_{w}

where
P_{v,v}(q)=1
and when
w\prec v
, the polynomial
P_{w,v}(q)\in{\mathbb{Z}}[q]
has degree
<\frac{\ell(v)-\ell(w)-1}{2}
. The
inverse
Kazhdan-Luszitg polynomials
\mathbf{m}_{v}^{w}(q)
are then characterized by:

H_{w}=\sum_{v\preccurlyeq w}\epsilon(wv^{-1})\mathbf{m}_{v}^{w}(q)C_{v}.

We will be particularly interested in the special value
\mathbf{m}_{v}^{w}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbf{m}_{v}^{w}(1)
.

Remark 2.1
.

Unless otherwise specified,
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\pi^{-1}(e)
is the
scheme-theoretic
fiber, which in general may be non-reduced. For example, when
G
is of type D
4
and
e\in\mathfrak{g}
is subregular nilpotent, then
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
is the exceptional divisor of a the resolution of a type D
4
Kleinian singularity
X^{2}+Y^{3}+Z^{3}=0
, which is non-reduced.

2.2.
G^{\vee}
-equivariant line bundles on flag varieties

Definition 2.2
.

For
\gamma\in Q^{\vee}
, let
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{B}}(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}G^{\vee}\times^{B_{0}^{\vee}}{\mathbb{C}}_{-\gamma}
, a
G^{\vee}
-equivariant line bundle on
{\mathscr{B}}
. For a locally closed subset
X\subset{\mathscr{B}}
, we let
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{B}}(\gamma)|_{X}
.

In fact, all
G^{\vee}
-equivariant line bundles on
{\mathscr{B}}
are obtained this way:

Lemma 2.3
.

\gamma\mapstochar\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{B}}(\gamma)
gives an isomorphism
\Lambda\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Pic}^{G^{\vee}}\!({\mathscr{B}})
.

Example 2.4
.

For each
i\in I
, there is a
G^{\vee}
-equivariant line bundle on
{\mathscr{B}}
whose fiber over
\mathfrak{b}\in{\mathscr{B}}
is
\pi_{i}(\mathfrak{b})/\mathfrak{b}
. This is
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{B}}(\alpha_{i}^{\vee})
.

Lemma 2.5
.

For each
i\in I
, the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{B}}(\gamma)
restricted to
\pi_{i}^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}_{i})\simeq\mathbf{P}^{1}
is
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(\langle\gamma,\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\rangle)
.

2.3.
Equivariant line bundles on
\mathbf{P}^{1}

{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
-equivariant line bundles on
\mathbf{P}^{1}
are given by, for
a_{1},a_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

(4)

{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(a_{1},a_{2})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}\times^{B}{\mathbb{C}}_{(a_{1},a_{2})},

where
{\mathbb{C}}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}
is the one-dimensional
B
-representation where
\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}&*\\
&\alpha_{2}\end{pmatrix}\in B
acts as
\alpha_{1}^{a_{1}}\alpha_{2}^{a_{2}}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}\times^{B}{\mathbb{C}}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}
is
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{C}}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}
modulo the relation
(gb,x)\sim(g,b\cdot x)
, for
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
,
b\in B
, and
x\in{\mathbb{C}}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}
. The underlying line bundle of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(a_{1},a_{2})
is
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(a_{2}-a_{1})
.

Remark 2.6
.

Viewing
\mathbf{P}^{1}
as the space of lines
\ell\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
, there are two natural
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-equivariant line bundles on it:

•

the stalk at
\ell
is
\ell
: this is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(1,0)
.

•

the stalk at
\ell
is
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}/\ell
: this is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(0,1)
.

Thus,
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(a_{1},a_{2})
is isomorphic to the line bundle whose stalk at
\ell\in\mathbf{P}^{1}
is
\ell^{\otimes a_{1}}\otimes({\mathbb{C}}^{2}/\ell)^{\otimes a_{2}}
.

The global sections of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(a_{1},a_{2})
can be explicitly computed as a
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
-representation:

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
a_{1},a_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
be such that
a_{1}\leqslant a_{2}
. Then letting
V
be the tautological
2
-dimensional representation of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
,

H^{0}(\mathbf{P}^{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(a_{1},a_{2}))\simeq\det(V)^{\otimes-a_{2}}\otimes\mathrm{Sym}^{a_{2}-a_{1}}(V).

Proof.

Global sections of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(a_{1},a_{2})
are functions
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}\rightarrow{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}\times^{B}{\mathbb{C}}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}:g\mapstochar\rightarrow(g,f(g))
, which satisfies
(gb,f(gb))=(g,f(g))
for all
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
and
b\in B
. In other words, it is the space of functions
f\colon{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}
such that
f(gb)=b^{-1}\cdot f(g)
for all
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
and
b\in B
. A basis for
H^{0}(\mathbf{P}^{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(a_{1},a_{2}))
is given by, for
g=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}
,

f(g)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\det(g)^{-a_{2}}\alpha^{i}\gamma^{j},

where
i
and
j
are non-negative such that
i+j=a_{2}-a_{1}
.
∎

Example 2.8
.

The canonical bundle on
\mathbf{P}^{1}
is
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(1,-1)
: the underlying bundle being
{\mathcal{O}}(-2)
and the
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
-equivariance descending to
{\mathrm{PGL}}_{2}
-equivariance uniquely characterizes it.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
n\geqslant 0
, and let
V
be a
2
-dimensional vector space.
Then, in
K^{{\mathrm{GL}}(V)}(\mathbf{P}_{V})
,

[\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(V)\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{V}}]\simeq\sum_{i=0}^{n}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{V}}(i,n-i)]

Proof.

After taking the image of
[\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(V)\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{V}}]
under the isomorphism
K^{{\mathrm{GL}}(V)}(\mathbf{P}_{V})\simeq K^{T}(*)
for a maximal torus
T\subset{\mathrm{GL}}(V)
, this is simply the weight space decomposition.
∎

Remark 2.10
.

Consider the orthogonal group
{\mathrm{O}}_{2}\subset{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
, which we identify with the semidirect product
\mathbb{G}_{m}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
.
We will eventually restrict the equality in Lemma
2.9
to
K^{\mathbb{G}_{m}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2}(\mathbf{P}^{1})
. Then, it will be useful to observe that as
\mathbb{G}_{m}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-representations:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sym}^{2n}(V)

\displaystyle\simeq 1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{2i}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sym}^{2n-1}(V)

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{2i-1}.

As a corollary of Proposition
2.7
, we obtain the following classification of
{\mathrm{O}}_{2}
-equivariant line bundles on
\mathbf{P}^{1}
:

Corollary 2.11
.

The
\mathbb{G}_{m}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-equivariant line bundles on
\mathbf{P}^{1}
are of the form
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(-m,0)
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(-m+1,1)
. In particular, there is an isomorphism

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Pic}^{\mathbb{G}_{m}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2}(\mathbf{P}^{1})

\displaystyle(m,0)

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(-m,0)

\displaystyle(m,1)

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(-m+1,1).

Moreover, the global sections are given by, for
m\geqslant 0
:

\displaystyle H^{0}(\mathbf{P}^{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(-m,0))

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\xi_{2i}&m\equiv 0\pmod{2}\\
\sum_{i=0}^{(m-1)/2}\xi_{2i+1}&m\equiv 1\pmod{2}\end{cases}

\displaystyle H^{0}(\mathbf{P}^{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(-m+1,1))

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}\operatorname{sgn}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\xi_{2i}&m\equiv 0\pmod{2}\\
\sum_{i=0}^{(m-1)/2}\xi_{2i+1}&m\equiv 1\pmod{2}\end{cases}

where
\xi_{m}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}^{{\mathrm{O}}_{2}}{\mathbb{C}}\langle m\rangle
are irreducible representations of
{\mathrm{O}}_{2}
.

Proof.

The first half is clear. For the second half, it suffices to compute
\mathrm{Sym}^{m}(\xi_{1})
. Note that the character of
\xi_{i}
is

\displaystyle\tr\xi_{1}(z,1)

\displaystyle=z^{i}+z^{-i}

\displaystyle\tr\xi_{1}(z,-1)

\displaystyle=0.

Now, the character of
\mathrm{Sym}^{m}(\xi_{1})
is

\displaystyle\tr\xi_{1}(z,1)

\displaystyle=z^{m}+z^{m-2}+\cdots+z^{-m}

\displaystyle\tr\xi_{1}(z,-1)

\displaystyle=1+(-1)^{m},

which matches the character of the expressions in the Corollary.
∎

2.4.
Explicit description of non-simply laced root systems

Throughout the paper, we will rely on the following explicit description of root systems in types B, C, F
4
, and G
2
:

Example 2.12
.

When
G
is of type
\text{B}_{n}
and
G^{\vee}
is of type
\text{C}_{n}
, the dual root system is:

\displaystyle V^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathbb{R}}\{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n}\}

\displaystyle\Phi^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\pm\epsilon_{i}\pm\epsilon_{j}:1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant n\}\cup\{\pm 2\epsilon_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant n\}

\displaystyle\Delta^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2},\dots,\epsilon_{n-1}-\epsilon_{n}\}\cup\{2\epsilon_{n}\}.

Let
\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{i+1}
for
i\leqslant n-1
and
\beta^{\vee}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}2\epsilon_{n}
. The highest short coroot is

\theta^{\vee}=\alpha_{1}^{\vee}+2\alpha_{2}^{\vee}+\cdots+2\alpha_{n-1}^{\vee}+\beta^{\vee}=\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}.

Example 2.13
.

When
G
is of type
\text{C}_{n}
and
G^{\vee}
is of type
\text{B}_{n}
, the dual root system is:

\displaystyle V^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathbb{R}}\{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n}\}

\displaystyle\Phi^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\pm\epsilon_{i}\pm\epsilon_{j}:1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant n\}\cup\{\pm\epsilon_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant n\}

\displaystyle\Delta^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2},\dots,\epsilon_{n-1}-\epsilon_{n}\}\cup\{\epsilon_{n}\}.

Let
\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{i+1}
for
i\leqslant n-1
and
\beta^{\vee}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\epsilon_{n}
. The highest short root is

\theta^{\vee}=\alpha_{1}^{\vee}+\cdots+\alpha_{n-1}^{\vee}+\beta^{\vee}=\epsilon_{1}.

Example 2.14
.

When
G
and
G^{\vee}
are of type F
4
,

\displaystyle V^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathbb{R}}\{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4}\}

\displaystyle\Phi^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\pm\epsilon_{i},\pm\epsilon_{i}\pm\epsilon_{j}:i\neq j\}\cup\Big\{\frac{1}{2}(\pm\epsilon_{1}\pm\epsilon_{2}\pm\epsilon_{3}\pm\epsilon_{4})\Big\}

\displaystyle\Delta^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\alpha_{1}^{\vee},\alpha_{2}^{\vee},\alpha_{3}^{\vee},\alpha_{4}^{\vee}\},

where
\alpha_{1}^{\vee}=\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}–\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{4}
,
\alpha_{2}^{\vee}=2\epsilon_{4}
,
\alpha_{3}^{\vee}=\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{4}
,
\alpha_{4}^{\vee}=\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3}
. The highest short root is

\theta^{\vee}=\alpha_{1}^{\vee}+3\alpha_{2}^{\vee}+2\alpha_{3}^{\vee}+\alpha_{4}^{\vee}=\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}.

Example 2.15
.

When
G
and
G^{\vee}
are of type G
2
,

\displaystyle V^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathbb{R}}\{\alpha^{\vee},\beta^{\vee}\}

\displaystyle\Phi^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\pm\alpha^{\vee},\pm\beta^{\vee},\pm(\alpha^{\vee}+\beta^{\vee}),\pm(2\alpha^{\vee}+\beta^{\vee}),\pm(3\alpha^{\vee}+\beta^{\vee}),\pm(3\alpha+2\beta^{\vee})\}

\displaystyle\Delta^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\alpha^{\vee},\beta^{\vee}\},

where the inner product on
V^{\vee}
is given by
(\alpha^{\vee},\alpha^{\vee})=2,(\alpha^{\vee},\beta^{\vee})=-3,(\beta^{\vee},\beta^{\vee})=6
. The highest short root is

\theta^{\vee}=2\alpha^{\vee}+\beta^{\vee}
.

2.5.
Folding Dynkin diagrams

Let
\widetilde{\Delta}^{\vee}\subset\widetilde{\Phi}^{\vee}
be a simple, simply-laced root system (i.e., of type A, D, or E) in a Euclidean vector space
\widetilde{V}^{\vee}
, and let
\Gamma
be a finite group of automorphisms of the root system (equivalently, graph-theoretic automorphisms of the Dynkin diagram). We assume without loss that
(\widetilde{\alpha}^{\vee},\widetilde{\alpha}^{\vee})=2
for all
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\vee}\in\widetilde{\Delta}^{\vee}
. Assume further that for any
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\vee}\in\widetilde{\Delta}^{\vee}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\vee}\neq\gamma(\widetilde{\alpha}^{\vee})
, the two coroots
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\vee}
and
\gamma(\widetilde{\alpha}^{\vee})
are orthogonal to each other.

Then we define the
folding
of the root system
\widetilde{\Delta}^{\vee}
as follows: for each

\Gamma
-orbit
\zeta\in\Gamma\backslash\widetilde{\Delta}^{\vee}
, let

\alpha_{\zeta}^{\vee}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{\widetilde{\alpha}^{\vee}\in\zeta}\widetilde{\alpha}^{\vee},

which has length
2|\zeta|
, by our orthogonality assumption. Now let
\Delta^{\vee}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\alpha_{\zeta}^{\vee}:\zeta\in\Gamma\backslash\widetilde{\Delta}^{\vee}\}
, which form a basis of the root system
\Phi^{\vee}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\widetilde{\Phi}^{\vee})^{\Gamma}=\{\widetilde{\alpha}^{\vee}\in\widetilde{\Phi}^{\vee}:\gamma(\widetilde{\alpha}^{\vee})=\widetilde{\alpha}^{\vee}\text{ for all }\gamma\in\Gamma\}
, in the Euclidean space
V^{\vee}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\widetilde{V}^{\vee})^{\Gamma}
.

Moreover, the highest short coroot
\theta^{\vee}\in\Phi^{\vee}
equals the highest coroot
\theta^{\vee}\in\widetilde{\Phi}^{\vee}
.

Any simple Lie algebra can be realized as the folding of a simply-laced Lie algebra:
\text{C}_{n}
is the folding of
\text{A}_{2n-1}
and G
2
is the folding of D
4
. Thus, we realize our root system of
G^{\vee}
as the folding of a simply-laced adjoint reductive group
\widetilde{G}^{\vee}
by some automorphism group
\Gamma
.

Example 2.16
.

Let
\widetilde{\Delta}^{\vee}\subset\widetilde{\Phi}^{\vee}
be of type
{\text{D}}_{n+1}
, where
n\geqslant 3
. Explicitly:

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathbb{R}}\{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n+1}\}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\pm\epsilon_{i}\pm\epsilon_{j}:1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant n+1\}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2},\dots,\epsilon_{n-1}-\epsilon_{n}\}\cup\{\epsilon_{n}\pm\epsilon_{n+1}\}.

Then
\Gamma=\{1,\sigma\}
where
\sigma(\epsilon_{i})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\epsilon_{i}
for
i\leqslant n
and
\sigma(\epsilon_{n+1})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}-\epsilon_{n+1}
satisfies the conditions above. The folding then gives a root system
\Delta^{\vee}\subset\Phi^{\vee}
of type
\text{C}_{n}
is as in Example
2.12
.

Example 2.17
.

Let
\widetilde{\Delta}^{\vee}\subset\widetilde{\Phi}^{\vee}
be of type
A_{2n-1}
, so:

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{2n}a_{i}\delta_{i}:\sum_{i=1}^{2n}a_{i}=0\Big\}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\{\delta_{i}-\delta_{i+1}:0<i<2n\}.

Then
\Gamma=\{1,\sigma\}
where
\sigma(\delta_{i})=-\delta_{2n+1-i}
. Then the folding of the root system is given by:

\displaystyle V^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}(\delta_{i}-\delta_{2n+1-i})\Big\}

\displaystyle\Delta^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\{\delta_{i}-\delta_{i+1}+\delta_{2n-i}-\delta_{2n+1-i}:0<i<n\}\cup\{\delta_{n}-\delta_{n+1}\},

which is identified with the root system of Example
2.13
of type
\text{B}_{n}
, by
\epsilon_{i}=\delta_{i}-\delta_{2n+1-i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.

Example 2.18
.

Recall that explicitly, the E
6
lattice is

\widetilde{Q}^{\vee}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{8}a_{i}\delta_{i}:\text{either all }a_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\text{ or all }a_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}+\frac{1}{2},\text{ and }\sum_{i=1}^{6}a_{i}=a_{7}+a_{8}=0\Big\},

with root system

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}^{\vee}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}

\displaystyle\Big\{\delta_{i}-\delta_{j}:i\neq j,\text{ either }i,j\notin\{7,8\}\text{ or }i,j\in\{7,8\}\Big\}

\displaystyle\cup\Big\{\frac{1}{2}(\pm\delta_{1}\cdots\pm\delta_{8}):\text{3 minus signs among }\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{6}\text{ and 1 minus sign among }\delta_{7},\delta_{8}\Big\},

with simple roots

with Dynkin diagram:

\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{6}=2\alpha_{5}^{\vee}+3\widetilde{\alpha}_{4}^{\vee}+2\widetilde{\alpha}_{3}^{\vee}+2\widetilde{\gamma}^{\vee}+\widetilde{\alpha}_{2}^{\vee}+\widetilde{\gamma}^{\vee}
\widetilde{\alpha}_{2}^{\vee}
\widetilde{\alpha}_{5}^{\vee}
\widetilde{\alpha}_{3}^{\vee}
\widetilde{\alpha}_{4}^{\vee}
\widetilde{\gamma}^{\vee}
\widetilde{\beta}^{\vee}

Thus, the folded root system
\Phi^{\vee}
has simple roots:

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\alpha}_{2}^{\vee}+\widetilde{\beta}^{\vee}=\delta_{2}-\delta_{3}+\delta_{7}-\delta_{8}

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\alpha}_{3}^{\vee}+\widetilde{\gamma}^{\vee}=\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{1}-\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}-3\delta_{4}+\delta_{5}+\delta_{6}-\delta_{7}+\delta_{8})

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\alpha}_{4}^{\vee}=\delta_{4}-\delta_{5}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\alpha}_{5}^{\vee}=\delta_{5}-\delta_{6}.

2.6.
The Springer resolution

Since the projection to the second factor
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}\rightarrow{\mathscr{B}}
is
G^{\vee}
-equivariant, the
G^{\vee}
-equivariant line bundles
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{B}}(\gamma)
pullback to
G^{\vee}
-equivariant line bundles
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}(\gamma)
.

For each
\alpha\in\Delta
, as in
[
BKK24
, §4.1]
let
\iota_{\alpha}
be the closed immersion (an inclusion of vector bundles over
{\mathscr{B}}
)

(5)

\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}T^{*}{\mathscr{P}}_{\alpha}\times_{{\mathscr{P}}_{\alpha}}{\mathscr{B}}=\{(e,\mathfrak{b}^{\vee})\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\times{\mathscr{B}}:e\in\pi_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{b}^{\vee})^{\mathrm{nil}}\}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}=T^{*}{\mathscr{B}}.

Lemma 2.20

(
[
Bez06
, Equation (13)]
or
[
Ach12
, Lem 5.3]
)
.

For each
\alpha\in\Delta
, there is a
G^{\vee}
-equivariant short exact sequence

0\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}(\alpha^{\vee})\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}\rightarrow i_{\alpha*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}}\rightarrow 0.

2.7.
Kleinian singularities and Slodowy slices

Let
\{e,h,f\}
be a
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
-triple in
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
such that
e\in{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
. Then let
S\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(e+{\mathrm{Z}}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}(f))\cap{\mathcal{N}}
, which is a surface with a Kleinian singularity and has a resolution of singularities
\pi_{S}\colon\widetilde{S}\rightarrow S
. Here
S
carries the action of
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathrm{Z}}_{G^{\vee}}(e,f)
. Since
\pi_{S}
is given by a sequence of blow-ups, the exceptional divisor
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
is the union of
\mathbf{P}^{1}
’s. The
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
-orbits of irreducible components of
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
can be explicitly described:

Lemma 2.21
.

For
e\in{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
, the Springer fiber
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\mathfrak{b}\in{\mathscr{B}}:e\in\mathfrak{b}\}
has a (set-theoretic) decomposition

{\mathscr{B}}_{e}=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\Delta}\big({\mathscr{B}}_{e}\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}\big),

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}
is defined in (
5
). Here

{\mathscr{B}}_{e}\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}=\pi_{\alpha}^{-1}(S_{\alpha})

for
S_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\mathfrak{p}_{\alpha}^{\vee}\in{\mathscr{P}}_{\alpha}:e\in(\mathfrak{p}_{\alpha}^{\vee})^{\mathrm{nil}}\}\subset{\mathscr{P}}_{\alpha}
is a finite set with a transitive
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
-action. In other words,
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
is a finite union of
\mathbf{P}^{1}
’s.

Let
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\pi_{\alpha}^{-1}(S_{\alpha})
, a disjoint union of
\mathbf{P}^{1}
’s, so set-theoretically,
1
1

1

As noted in Remark
2.1
,
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
may be non-reduced.

{\mathscr{B}}_{e}=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\Delta}\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}
. Now, the intersection multiplicity is given as:

Lemma 2.22

(
[
Slo80
, §6.2]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a simple Lie algebra and let
\alpha,\beta\in\Delta
. Then
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\beta}=-(\alpha^{\vee},\beta^{\vee})
.

The sheaf of ideals defining
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}\subset\widetilde{S}
has the following explicit description:

Proposition 2.23
.

Let
\theta^{\vee}\in\Phi^{\vee}
be the highest short coroot. Then there is a short exact sequence of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{S}}
-modules

0\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{S}}(\theta^{\vee})\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{S}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}\rightarrow 0

Proof.

By
[
Art66
, Theorem 4]
for
n=1
, the sheaf of ideals defining
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}\subset\widetilde{S}
is
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{S}}(-Z_{\mathrm{num}})
, where
Z_{\mathrm{num}}
, the
numerical cycle
, is the unique minimal positive exceptional divisor supported on
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
such that
Z_{\mathrm{num}}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}\leqslant 0
for all
\alpha\in\Delta
. Using the formula
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\beta}=-(\alpha^{\vee},\beta^{\vee})
and Example
2.4
gives the desired result.
∎

Remark 2.24
.

\theta^{\vee}
is equivalently the dual of the highest root
\theta\in\Phi
.

In fact, the exact sequence of Proposition
2.23
extends to
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
:

Corollary 2.25
.

Let
\theta^{\vee}\in\Phi^{\vee}
be the highest short root. Then there is a
G^{\vee}
-equivariant short exact sequence of coherent sheaves on
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}

0\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}(\theta^{\vee})\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\overline{{\mathbb{O}}}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}\!\rightarrow 0.

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{\overline{{\mathbb{O}}}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
is a divisor it is defined by some line bundle
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}
. All
G^{\vee}
-equivariant line bundles on
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
must be of the form
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}(\gamma)
but by restricting to
\widetilde{S}
, and by comparing with Proposition
2.23
, we see that for all
\alpha\in\Delta
,
(\alpha^{\vee},\gamma)=(\alpha^{\vee},\theta^{\vee})
,
i.e.,
\gamma=\theta^{\vee}
.
∎

Remark 2.26
.

Since
s_{0}=t_{\theta^{\vee}}\cdot s_{\theta^{\vee}}
, the above shows that in
K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U})
,

s_{0}\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}]=t_{\theta^{\vee}}{s_{\theta^{\vee}}}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}]=-[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}(\theta^{\vee})]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}].

Another way to see this is to use Lemma
2.29
below.

As a consequence of Corollary
2.25
, we obtain the following:

Proposition 2.27
.

Using notation from
[
AB09
]
, consider Arkhipov and Bezrukavnikov’s equivalence

{{}^{f}}\Phi\colon D^{\mathrm{b}}\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}\big)\simeq D^{\mathrm{b}}\big({{}^{f}}\mathcal{P}_{I}\big),

and let
L_{s_{0}}=j_{s_{0}!*}(\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}[1])
. Then

{{}^{f}}\Phi\big({\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\overline{{\mathbb{O}}}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}[-1]\big)\simeq L_{s_{0}}.

In particular,
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\overline{{\mathbb{O}}}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}[-1]
is an irreducible exotic coherent sheaf.

Proof.

Since the stratum
\mathcal{F}\ell_{s_{0}}\subset\mathcal{F}\ell
has closure isomorphic to
\mathbf{P}^{1}
, there is an exact sequence

0\rightarrow L_{s_{0}}\rightarrow j_{s_{0}!}\rightarrow\delta_{e}\rightarrow 0,

and there is a one-dimensional space of homomorphisms
j_{s_{0}!}\rightarrow\delta_{e}
. Now under Arkhipov and Bezrukavnikov’s equivalence,
j_{s_{0}!}=j_{t_{\theta^{\vee}}!}
is sent to
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}(\theta^{\vee})
and
\delta_{e}
is sent to
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}
, so we obtain an exact sequence

(6)

0\rightarrow{{}^{f}}\Phi^{-1}(L_{s_{0}})\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}(\theta^{\vee})\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}\rightarrow 0.

Comparing this with the non-split exact triangle

{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\overline{{\mathbb{O}}}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}[-1]\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}(\theta^{\vee})\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}\xrightarrow{+1},

from Corollary
2.25
, we see that
{{}^{f}}\Phi^{-1}(L_{s_{0}})\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\overline{{\mathbb{O}}}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}[-1]
.
∎

2.8.
K
-theory

There is a standard short exact sequence of
{\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{W}\simeq K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}\times_{\mathcal{N}}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}})
-modules

0\rightarrow K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}})\rightarrow K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U})\rightarrow K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{reg}})\rightarrow 0.

Note that
\pi\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{N}}
is an isomorphism on the dense open subset
{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{reg}}\subset{\mathcal{N}}
, so
K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{reg}})\simeq K^{G^{\vee}}\!({\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{reg}})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}
. Moreover,
K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}})\simeq K^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}({\mathscr{B}}_{e})
, where
e\in{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
, and
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
is the reductive part of the centralizer of
e
in
G^{\vee}
(i.e., the centralizer of a
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
-triple completing
e
).

Lemma 2.28

(
[
Lus02
, Prop 2.9]
)
.

The
\widehat{W}
-action on
K^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}({\mathscr{B}}_{e})
is such that
\gamma\in\Lambda\subset\widehat{W}
acts as
-\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}(\gamma)
.

Lemma
2.20
allows us to inductively calculate the
Q^{\vee}
-action on
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}
:

Lemma 2.29
.

For all
\alpha^{\vee}\in\Delta^{\vee}
and
\gamma\in Q^{\vee}
,

[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}(\gamma+\alpha^{\vee})]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}(\gamma)]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}}(\gamma)]
in
K^{G^{\vee}}(\widetilde{U})
.

2.9.
Representations of the affine Lie algebra
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}

Let
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{g}[t^{\pm 1}]\oplus{\mathbb{C}}K\oplus{\mathbb{C}}d
be the affine Lie algebra associated to
\mathfrak{g}
, with root system
\widehat{\Phi}
. Then
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
admits a standard direct sum decomposition
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}=\widehat{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}\oplus\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}\oplus\widehat{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}
, where
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{h}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}K\oplus{\mathbb{C}}d
, and
\widehat{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{n}_{+}\oplus\bigoplus_{n>0}\mathfrak{g}t^{n}
, and
\widehat{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{n}_{-}\oplus\bigoplus_{n<0}\mathfrak{g}t^{n}
. Let
\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}\oplus\widehat{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}
. For any character
\Lambda\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}
, extending trivially to
\widehat{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}
gives a character
\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
. Now, the associated
Verma module
is

M(\Lambda)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}U(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U(\widehat{\mathfrak{b}})}{\mathbb{C}}_{\Lambda},

which has a unique irreducible quotient
L(\Lambda)
.

Definition 2.30
.

For
\Lambda\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}
, let
\kappa\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Lambda(K)
, the scalar by which the (central) element
K\in\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
acts on
L(\Lambda)
.

Let
\widehat{\Delta}
be the set of simple roots of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Set
\widehat{Q}^{+}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i\in\widehat{\Delta}}{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geqslant 0}\alpha_{i}
. The above representations naturally live in the following category:

Definition 2.31
.

Fix a
\kappa>-h^{\vee}
, the dual Coxeter number. The category
{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}
is the full subcategory of the category of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules of level
\kappa
, consisting of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules
M
such that

(1)

M=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}}M_{\mu}
, where
M_{\mu}
is a generalized
\mu
-weight space for
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
;

(2)

each
M_{\mu}
are finite-dimensional; and

(3)

for any
\mu\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}
there exists only finitely many
\beta\in\mu+\widehat{Q}^{+}
such that
M_{\beta}\neq 0
.

For an object in the category
{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}
, we can define the character:

Definition 2.32
.

For an object
M\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}
, let the
character
of
M
be

\operatorname{ch}M\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{\mu\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}}(\dim M_{\mu})\cdot e^{\mu},

which is an element of the group of formal power series
{\mathbb{C}}[\![\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}]\!]\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\sum_{\mu\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}}a_{\mu}e^{\mu}:a_{\mu}\in{\mathbb{C}}\}
.

Moreover, for convenience, let
\widehat{R}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}e^{\widehat{\rho}}\prod_{\alpha\in\widehat{\Phi}_{+}}(1-e^{-\alpha})^{\mathrm{mult}(\alpha)}
, where
\widehat{\Phi}_{+}\subset\widehat{\Phi}
is the set of positive roots of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
. Moreover, let
\widehat{\rho}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=0}^{r}\Lambda_{i}
, where
\Lambda_{i}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}
are the fundamental weights. Then

\widehat{R}\operatorname{ch}M(\Lambda)=e^{\Lambda+\widehat{\rho}}.

3.
The subregular two-sided cell

The subregular two-sided cell
c_{\mathrm{subreg}}
and the left cell
c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
have the following description, by
[
Lus83
, Prop 3.8]
:

\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{subreg}}

\displaystyle=\{w\in\widehat{W}\setminus\{1\}:w\text{ has a unique reduced decomposition}\}

\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}

\displaystyle=c_{\mathrm{subreg}}\cap\widehat{W}^{f}=\{w\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}:\ell(ws_{0})<\ell(w)\}.

We first make some easy preliminary observations (see
[
Lus83
, Section 3.7]
):

Lemma 3.1
.

In a Coxeter group
\langle s_{1},\dots,s_{k}|(s_{i}s_{j})^{m_{ij}}=1\rangle
with
m_{ii}=1
and
m_{ij}\geqslant 2
for
i\neq j
, if
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{n}}
is a unique reduced decomposition, then:

(1)

For each
k\leqslant n-1
,
m_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}\geqslant 3
; and

(2)

No pattern of the form
s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}
appears for
i,j
such that
m_{ij}=3
, no pattern of the form
s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}
appears for
i,j
such that
m_{ij}=4
, etc.

In other words,
w
defines a path
(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
on the Coxeter-Dynkin diagram such that an edge
ij
appears
<m_{ij}-1
times consecutively.

Proof.

For (
1
), if
m_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}=2
then
s_{i_{k}}
and
s_{i_{k+1}}
commute with each other, so swapping
s_{i_{k}}
and
s_{i_{k+1}}
gives another presentation of
w
.

Similarly for (
2
), if
m_{ij}=3
then
s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}=s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}
, and
m_{ij}=4
then
s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}=s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}
, so the reduced decomposition is not unique.
∎

Now each element
w\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}
has a unique reduced expression of the form
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}s_{0}
. Following
[
Lus83
, §3.7]
Let
\Gamma_{0}
be the graph with underlying set
c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}
, and an edge
y\sim w
for
y,w\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}
such that there exists a simple reflection
s\in\widehat{S}
with
y=sw
.

There is a map

\displaystyle\mu\colon c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\widehat{S}=S\cup\{s_{0}\}

\displaystyle s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}s_{0}

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow s_{i_{1}},

characterized by
\mu(w)=s_{i}
for
w\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}
if and only if
\ell(s_{i}w)<\ell(w)
. Now
[
Lus83
, Prop 3.8]
defines the
\widehat{W}
-module
E^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
with
{\mathbb{Z}}
-basis
\{e_{w}:w\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}\}
: for
y\in\widehat{S}
,

(7)

t(e_{w})=\begin{cases}-e_{w}&\text{if }\mu(w)=t\\
\displaystyle e_{w}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0},\ \mu(y)=t\\
y,w\text{ edge of }\Gamma_{0}\end{subarray}}e_{y}&\text{if }\mu(w)\neq t.\end{cases}

Now, we define the following module, which will be used in our description of the
\widehat{W}
-module structure on
K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U})
:

Proposition-Definition 3.2
.

Let
\widetilde{E}^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
be the
\widehat{W}
-module with
{\mathbb{Z}}
-basis
\{e_{w}:w\in c_{\mathrm{reg}}\cup c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}\}=\{e_{1}\}\cup\{e_{w}:w\in c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}\}
such that
t(e_{w})
is defined as in (
7
) for
w\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}
and

(8)

t(e_{1})=\begin{cases}e_{1}+e_{s_{0}}&t=s_{0}\\
-e_{1}&t\in S.\end{cases}

The module
\widetilde{E}^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
is well-defined, and fits into a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow E^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}\rightarrow\widetilde{E}^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow 0.

Proof.

It suffices to check, for each
t\in S
, the braid relation of
t
and
s_{0}
acting on
e_{1}
, i.e., that

(9)

\underbrace{\cdots s_{0}ts_{0}}_{m_{s_{0},t}}(e_{1})=\underbrace{\cdots ts_{0}t}_{m_{s_{0},t}}(e_{1}).

An easy induction shows that for
n<m_{s_{0},t}
,

(10)

\underbrace{\cdots s_{0}ts_{0}}_{n}(e_{1})=e_{1}+e_{s_{0}}+\cdots+e_{\underbrace{\cdots s_{0}ts_{0}}_{n}}.

When
n=m_{s_{0},t}-1
, the above is exactly the right-hand side of (
9
). For
n=m_{s_{0},t}
, we apply
s_{0}
or
t
to (
10
) (depending on the parity of
m_{s_{0},t}
), to obtain

\underbrace{\cdots s_{0}ts_{0}}_{m_{s_{0},t}}(e_{1})=e_{1}+e_{s_{0}}+\cdots+e_{\underbrace{\cdots s_{0}ts_{0}}_{m_{s_{0},t}-1}},

using the observation that
w\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\underbrace{\cdots s_{0}ts_{0}}_{m_{s_{0},t}}
is not in
c_{\mathrm{subreg}}
, since the braid relation (
9
) gives two reduced expressions for
w
.
∎

Lusztig actually defines a representation of the affine Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{q}(\widehat{W})
, whose specialization is
E^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
. The extension
\widetilde{E}^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
can also be defined as a
\mathcal{H}_{q}(\widehat{W})
-module. More generally, one can define the following bimodule (whose well-definedness follows from the same argument as in Proposition-Definition
3.2
):

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\widetilde{E}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
be the
\mathcal{H}_{q}(\widehat{W})
-bimodule with
{\mathbb{Z}}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
-basis
\{e_{w}:w\geq_{LR}c_{\mathrm{subreg}}\}=\{e_{1}\}\cup\{e_{w}:w\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}\}
such that for
w\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}
and
t\in\widehat{S}
,

\displaystyle T_{t}(e_{w})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-e_{w}&\text{if }\ell(tw)<\ell(w)\\
\displaystyle qe_{w}+q^{1/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}},ty<y,\\
y=sw\text{ for some }s\in\widehat{S}\end{subarray}}e_{y}&\text{if }\ell(tw)>\ell(w).\end{cases}

\displaystyle(e_{w})T_{t}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-e_{w}&\text{if }\ell(wt)<\ell(w)\\
\displaystyle qe_{w}+q^{1/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}},yt<y,\\
y=ws\text{ for some }s\in\widehat{S}\end{subarray}}e_{y}&\text{if }\ell(wt)>\ell(w),\end{cases}

and

T_{t}\cdot e_{1}=e_{1}\cdot T_{t}=qe_{1}+q^{1/2}e_{t}.

Let
E_{\mathrm{subreg}}
be the sub-bimodule spanned by
\{e_{w}:w\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}\}
.

The
\mathcal{H}_{q}(\widehat{W})
-bimodule
\widetilde{E}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
contains
\widetilde{E}^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
as a left
\mathcal{H}_{q}(\widehat{W})
-submodule. Then we see:

Proposition 3.4
.

There is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{H}_{q}(\widehat{W})
-bimodules between
\widetilde{E}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
and

\mathcal{H}_{q}(\widehat{W})_{\geq_{LR}\mathrm{subreg}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{H}_{q}(\widehat{W})/\operatorname{Span}_{{\mathbb{Z}}[q^{\pm 1/2}]}(C_{v}\,|\,v\notin c_{\mathrm{subreg}}\cup\{1\})

and between
E_{\mathrm{subreg}}
and

\mathcal{H}_{q}(\widehat{W})_{\mathrm{subreg}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Span}_{{\mathbb{Z}}[q^{\pm 1/2}]}(C_{v}\,|\,v\neq 1)/\operatorname{Span}_{{\mathbb{Z}}[q^{\pm 1/2}]}(C_{v}\,|\,v\notin c_{\mathrm{subreg}}\cup\{1\}),

sending
e_{w}
to
C_{w}
.

Proof.

Follows from
[
Xu19
, Lemma 4.1]
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Although Proposition
3.4
provides an explicit description for the
\mathcal{H}
-bimodule
\mathcal{H}_{\geq_{LR}\mathrm{subreg}}
just using algebraic methods. However, in this paper we characterize the subregular anti-spherical module
geometrically
. Such a characterization has independent interest since it allows to
categorify
the canonical basis elements; we explicitly describe the corresponding irreducible perverse exotic sheaves in Section
4
. Moreover, the geometric approach also helps in computing Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials
\mathbf{m}_{v}^{w}
. Indeed, to compute them, we need to express
w(e_{1})\in\widetilde{E}^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
in terms of the canonical basis. To do so, we can write a reduced expression
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}
for
w
and compute
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}(e_{1})
. However, doing so naively does not give a nice general formula. Only by using our geometric characterization can we give general formulas. The second author plans to return to the problem of using Proposition
3.4
directly to compute
\mathbf{m}_{v}^{w}
for all
w\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}
, where geometric methods are not available.

3.1.
Explicit description of the subregular cell

Recall that
[
Lus83
, §3.13]
explicitly computes
c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}
, which we explicitly write as
w_{\nu}
for
\nu\in Q^{\vee}
. This is done by a case-work on the types:

3.2.
When
G
is of type B

Let
G
be of type
\text{B}_{n}
where
n\geqslant 3
, so
\widehat{W}=Q^{\vee}\rtimes W
is the affine Weyl group of type B. Explicitly,
W
is viewed as the group of signed permutations, i.e., permutations on
\{1,\dots,n,\overline{1},\dots,\overline{n}\}
fixing the subsets
\{i,\overline{i}\}
for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. Recall that the Coxeter-Dynkin diagram of
\widehat{W}=Q^{\vee}\rtimes W
is

Explicitly, the simple reflections in
Q^{\vee}\rtimes W
are:

\displaystyle s_{0}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}).(1\mapstochar\rightarrow\overline{1},2\mapstochar\rightarrow\overline{2})

\displaystyle s_{i}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(i,i+1)\text{ for }1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1

\displaystyle s_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(n\mapstochar\rightarrow\overline{n}).

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\widehat{W}=Q^{\vee}\rtimes W
be the affine Weyl group of type
\text{B}_{n}
where
n\geqslant 3
. Then the elements of
c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}
are one of:

(11)

\displaystyle s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}}

(12)

\displaystyle s_{1}s_{2}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}}

(13)

\displaystyle s_{i}s_{i-1}\cdots s_{2}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{i+1}}\text{ for }2\leqslant i<n

(14)

\displaystyle s_{i}\cdots s_{n-1}s_{n}s_{n-1}\cdots s_{2}s_{0}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}w_{\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{i}}\text{ for }2\leqslant i\leqslant n\\
w_{\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{1}}\text{ for }i=1\end{cases}

(15)

\displaystyle s_{0}s_{2}\cdots s_{n-1}s_{n}s_{n-1}\cdots s_{2}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{2\epsilon_{2}}.

The graph
\Gamma_{0}
is:

\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}
\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}
\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{3}
\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{4}
\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{3}
\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}
\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{1}
2\epsilon_{2}

3.3.
When
G
is of type C

When
\mathfrak{g}
is of type C, with affine Coxeter diagram labelled as follows:

4
4
s_{0}
s_{1}
s_{2}
s_{3}
s_{n-2}
s_{n-1}
s_{n}

Explicitly, write
Q^{\vee}=\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\epsilon_{i}:a_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
, with
W=({\mathbb{Z}}/2)^{n}\rtimes S_{n}
the group of signed permutations, so:

\displaystyle s_{0}

\displaystyle=(\epsilon_{1}).(1\mapstochar\rightarrow\overline{1})

\displaystyle s_{i}

\displaystyle=(i,i+1)\ \text{ for }\ 1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1

\displaystyle s_{n}

\displaystyle=(n\mapstochar\rightarrow\overline{n}).

Similar to type A
[
BKK24
, Cor 5.2, Lem 6.1]
, the subregular cell is infinite:

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
\widehat{W}=Q^{\vee}\rtimes W
be the affine Weyl group of type C. Then the elements of
c_{\mathrm{subreg}}\cap\widehat{W}^{f}
are either:

•

w_{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}}
; or

•

of the form
w_{k\epsilon_{i}}
where
k\neq 0\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.

The Bruhat-ordering is given by
w_{k\epsilon_{i}}\prec w_{\ell\epsilon_{j}}
if and only if
N_{k,i}<N_{\ell,j}
, where

N_{k,i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}2kn+1-i&k\equiv 0\mod 2,k>0\\
-2(k+1)n+i&k\equiv 0\mod 2,k<0\\
2(k-1)n+i&k\equiv 1\mod 2,k>0\\
-2kn+1-i&k\equiv 1\mod 2,k<0.\end{cases}

Proof.

Direct calculation shows:

\displaystyle s_{i}s_{i-1}\cdots s_{0}

\displaystyle=(\epsilon_{i+1}).(i+1,\overline{i},\dots,\overline{1})

\displaystyle s_{i}s_{i+1}\cdots s_{n}s_{n-1}\cdots s_{0}

\displaystyle=(-\epsilon_{i}).(i,i-1,\dots,1).

In particular, note that
s_{1}s_{2}\cdots s_{n-1}s_{n}s_{n-1}\cdots s_{0}=-\epsilon_{1}
. From Lemma
3.1
, all elements of
c_{\mathrm{subreg}}\cap\widehat{W}
are of the form (for some
N\geqslant 0
):

\displaystyle s_{0}s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=(\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2})(\overline{1},2)

\displaystyle s_{i}s_{i-1}\cdots s_{0}(s_{1}s_{2}\cdots s_{n}\cdots s_{0})^{N}

\displaystyle=(\epsilon_{i+1}).(i+1,\overline{i},\dots,\overline{1}).(-N\epsilon_{1})

\displaystyle=(N+1)\epsilon_{i+1}.(i+1,\overline{i},\dots,\overline{1})

\displaystyle s_{i}s_{i-1}\cdots s_{n}\cdots s_{0}(s_{1}s_{2}\cdots s_{n}\cdots s_{0})^{N}

\displaystyle=(-\epsilon_{i}).(i,i-1,\dots,1).(-N\epsilon_{1})

\displaystyle=(-(N+1)\epsilon_{i}).(i,i-1,\dots,1).

Now
N_{k,i}
counts the number of simple reflections in the reduced expression of
w_{k\epsilon_{i}}
, as can be checked by case-work.
∎

3.4.
When
G
is of type F
4

Analogously, in type F
4
, where the Coxeter diagram of the affine Weyl group is labelled as follows:

{}{}\hbox to124.9pt{\vbox to15.88pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.54382pt\lower-5.12961pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to0.0pt{{
{}{}{}}{}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 2.98 L 78.74 2.98}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \hss}{

We have:

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
\widehat{W}=Q^{\vee}\rtimes W
be the affine Weyl group of type F
4
. Then the elements of
c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}
are one of:

(16)

\displaystyle s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}}

(17)

\displaystyle s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{3}}

(18)

\displaystyle s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{4}}

(19)

\displaystyle s_{3}s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{4}}

(20)

\displaystyle s_{4}s_{3}s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}}

(21)

\displaystyle s_{2}s_{3}s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{3}}

(22)

\displaystyle s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}}

(23)

\displaystyle s_{0}s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{2\epsilon_{1}},

with graph
\Gamma_{0}
:

{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 125.98 2.98 L 188.98 2.98}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \hss}{

3.5.
When
G
is of type G
2

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
\widehat{W}=Q^{\vee}\rtimes W
be the affine Weyl group of type G
2
. Then the elements of
c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}
are one of:

(24)

\displaystyle s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{2\alpha^{\vee}+\beta^{\vee}}

(25)

\displaystyle s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{\alpha^{\vee}+\beta^{\vee}}

(26)

\displaystyle s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{\alpha^{\vee}}

(27)

\displaystyle s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{-\alpha^{\vee}}

(28)

\displaystyle s_{0}s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{3\alpha^{\vee}+\beta^{\vee}}

(29)

\displaystyle s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{-\alpha^{\vee}-\beta^{\vee}}

(30)

\displaystyle s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{-2\alpha^{\vee}-\beta^{\vee}}

(31)

\displaystyle s_{0}s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{4\alpha^{\vee}+2\beta^{\vee}},

with graph
\Gamma_{0}
:
