4.
Explicit description of the canonical basis

4.1.
Irreducible objects in the exotic
t
-structure in
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U}))

We start with the definition of the exotic
t
-structures we are dealing with. For a group
H
and an
H
-equivariant quasi-coherent sheaf of algebras
\mathcal{A}
on some variety
X
we will denote by
\mathcal{A}-\operatorname{mod}^{H}
the category of finitely generated
H
-equivariant quasi-coherent sheaves of
\mathcal{A}
-modules on
X
.

Theorem 4.1
.

(see
[
BKK24
, Theorem 4.5]
)
There exists a
G^{\vee}
-equivariant vector bundle
\mathcal{E}
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
with the following properties.

(1)

The structure sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{N}}}}
is a direct summand in
\mathcal{E}
. Also,
\mathcal{E}^{*}
is globally generated.

(2)

Let
A\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{End}(\mathcal{E})
. Then the functor
\mathcal{F}\mapstochar\rightarrow\operatorname{RHom}(\mathcal{E}^{*},\mathcal{F})
provides equivalence

D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{{\mathcal{N}}}))\simeq D^{\mathrm{b}}(A\mathrm{-mod}^{G^{\vee}}).

(3)

Irreducible objects in the heart of the exotic
t
-structure are in bijection with pairs
({\mathbb{O}},M)
where
{\mathbb{O}}
is a
G^{\vee}
-orbit in
{\mathcal{N}}
and
M
is an irreducible
G^{\vee}
-equivariant module for
A|_{{\mathbb{O}}}
. The corresponding irreducible object is supported on
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
.

By
[
BM13
, §6.2]
, the perverse exotic
t
-structure in
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{N}}}))
is the image of the perverse coherent
t
-structure in
D^{\mathrm{b}}(A\mathrm{-mod}^{G^{\vee}})
of middle perversity. The same description holds upon restricting to
U\subset{\mathcal{N}}
. Abusing notations, we denote the natural morphism
\widetilde{U}\rightarrow U
by
\pi
.
Set
\mathcal{E}_{\widetilde{U}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{E}|_{\widetilde{U}}
and
A_{U}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\pi_{*}End(\mathcal{E}_{\widetilde{U}})
(considered as a sheaf of algebras on
U
). Then the functor
\mathcal{F}\mapstochar\rightarrow\pi_{*}(RHom(\mathcal{E}_{U}^{*},\mathcal{F}))
provides the derived equivalence

(32)

D^{\mathrm{b}}\big(\!\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U})\big)\simeq D^{\mathrm{b}}(A_{U}\mathrm{-mod}^{G^{\vee}}),

which sends the exotic
t
-structure on the left to the perverse coherent
t
-structure on the right.

Let
D^{\mathrm{b}}(A_{\mathbb{O}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}\mathrm{-mod}^{G^{\vee}})
be the subcategory of
D^{\mathrm{b}}(A_{U}\mathrm{-mod}^{G^{\vee}})
consisting of complexes of finitely generated
A_{U}
-modules on
U
that are set-theoretically supported on
\mathbb{O}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
.
The equivalence (
32
) induces the equivalence

D^{\mathrm{b}}\big(\!\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}\!(\widetilde{U})\big)\simeq D^{\mathrm{b}}(A_{\mathbb{O}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}\mathrm{-mod}^{G^{\vee}}),

where
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}(\widetilde{U}))
is the subcategory of
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U}))
consisting of complexes that are set-theoretically supported on
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
.

Recall that we have a short exact sequence of categories

0\rightarrow D^{\mathrm{b}}\big(\!\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}(\widetilde{U})\big)\rightarrow D^{\mathrm{b}}\big(\!\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U})\big)\rightarrow D^{\mathrm{b}}\big(\!\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{reg}})\big)\rightarrow 0

compatible with the exotic
t
-structures (see
[
BM13
, Section 6.2.2]
). It is easy to see that the object
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}\in D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U}))
is an irreducible object of the exotic
t
-structure. All other irreducible objects lie in
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}(\widetilde{U}))
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Every irreducible object of the perverse exotic
t
-structure in
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}(\widetilde{U}))
is scheme-theoretically supported on
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
i.e., it lies in
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}))
.

Proof.

Follows from Theorem
4.1
(
3
) together with the fact that
\mathbb{O}_{\mathrm{subreg}}\subset U
is closed.
∎

Let
\widehat{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}
,
\widehat{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{e}}
be the formal neighbourhoods of
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{e}
as in
[
BM13
, Section 0.1]
. Set
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{E}|_{\widehat{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}}
,
\widehat{A_{e}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{End}(\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}})
.
By
[
BM13
, Section 5.2.1]
, we have the equivalence

D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}(\widehat{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}))\simeq D^{\mathrm{b}}(\widehat{A_{e}}\mathrm{-mod}^{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}).

This equivalence defines exotic
t
-structure in
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}(\widehat{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}))
.

Lemma 4.3
.

There exists an equivalence

D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}(\widehat{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}))\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}}}\,D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widehat{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{e}})),\,L\mapstochar\rightarrow\operatorname{Ind}_{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}^{G^{\vee}}L\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}G^{\vee}\times^{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}L.

This equivalence is compatible with exotic
t
-structures.

Proof.

The first claim is standard, compatibility with
t
-structures follows from the definitions.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

The forgetful functor
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}(\widehat{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}))\rightarrow D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}(\widehat{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}))
is bijective on irreducible objects (of the corresponding exotic
t
-structures).

Proof.

Follows from
[
BM13
, Section 6.3.2]
.
∎

So, our goal is to describe irreducible objects in
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}({\mathscr{B}}_{e}))\hookrightarrow D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}(\widehat{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}))
.
It follows from Lemma
4.4
that the corresponding irreducible objects in
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}({\mathscr{B}}_{e}))
will be the same complexes with additional equivariance (that we explicitly describe case-by-case in §
4.2
, §
4.3
, §
4.4
, and §
4.5
). The corresponding irreducibles in
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{e}))
are
\operatorname{Ind}^{G^{\vee}}_{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}L
(see Lemma
4.3
).

Recall that
\widetilde{S}
is the Slodowy variety. Let
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}(\widetilde{S}))
be the category of coherent
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
-equivariant sheaves on
\widetilde{S}
that are set-theoretically supported on
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
, equipped with the exotic
t
-structure (as in
[
BM13
, Section 5.2.1]
). We have an embedding
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}({\mathscr{B}}_{e}))\hookrightarrow D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}(\widetilde{S}))
inducing bijection on irreducible objects. So, it remains to describe irreducible objects in
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}(\widetilde{S}))
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
{\mathscr{P}}
be a
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
-equivariant vector bundle on
\widetilde{S}
such that the structure sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{S}}
is a direct summand in
{\mathscr{P}}
,
{\mathscr{P}}^{*}
is globally generated, and
{\mathscr{P}}
is a tilting generator. Then, the irreducible objects of the induced
t
-structure in
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}(\widetilde{S}))
are as in Propositions
4.9
,
4.18
,
4.21
,
4.24
.

Proof.

The argument is similar to the one in
[
BKK24
, Lemma 4.7]
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{e}\subset D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}(\widetilde{S}))
be the heart of the exotic
t
-structure. Let
\mathcal{A}^{0}_{e}\subset\mathcal{A}_{e}
be the subcategory consisting of objects
\mathcal{F}
such that
R\Gamma(\mathcal{F})=0
. This is indeed a subcategory because
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{S}}
is the direct summand of
{\mathscr{P}}
.

Note now that all our candidates for simple objects indeed lie in
\mathcal{A}_{e}
as after forgetting the equivariance, the corresponding objects lie in heart (use
[
BKK24
, Lemma 4.7]
) and the functor providing equivalence is the same. To see that the candidates that we have lying in
\mathcal{A}_{e}
are indeed simple it is enough to note that every object of
\mathcal{A}_{e}^{0}
is of the form
\mathcal{F}[1]
, where
\mathcal{F}
is an extension of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}}
(compare with the proof of
[
BKK24
, Lemma 4.7]
).

Now it remains to describe irreducible objects of
\mathcal{A}_{e}
that do not lie in
\mathcal{A}^{0}_{e}
. Note that as in the proof of
[
BKK24
, Lemma 4.7]
we can count the number of them (up to a twist by
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}^{0}
that appears only in type
C
) and the answer will be equal precisely to the number of
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
-equivariant sheaves of the form
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}\otimes?
that we have in Propositions
4.9
,
4.18
,
4.21
,
4.24
.

It remains to check that all of the objects above are indeed irreducible. Note that the functor
\operatorname{Forg}\colon D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}(\widetilde{S}))\rightarrow D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}(\widetilde{S}))
forgetting the equivariance is
t
-exact, so if object
L
is not irreducible, then
\operatorname{Forg}(L)
is also not irreducible. The only problem may appear when we deal with
L={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}\otimes\xi
, where
\xi
is a two-dimensional irreducible representation of
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
. In this case,
\operatorname{Forg}(L)={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}^{\oplus 2}
so the only proper submodule of
\operatorname{Forg}(L)
is
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}
. Let
F
be
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}
with any equivariant structure. It remains to check that every
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
-equivariant map
F\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}\otimes\xi
is equal to zero. First of all this map is determined by the map it induces on global sections (we use that
\operatorname{Hom}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}})=\mathbb{C}
). On global sections, we obtain a map from some one-dimensional representation of
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
to the irreducible two-dimensional representation
\xi
. Any such map should be zero.
To see that
\operatorname{Hom}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}})=\mathbb{C}
let us rewrite it as
\operatorname{Hom}(\pi^{*}\mathbb{C}_{e},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}})=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}_{e},\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}})=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C})
as global sections of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}
is
\mathbb{C}
(see the end of the proof of
[
BKK24
, Lemma 4.7]
).
∎

4.2.
When
G
is of type B

Let
G
be of type
\text{B}_{n}
where
n\geqslant 3
, so
G^{\vee}
is of type
\text{C}_{n}
. Let
G^{\vee}={\mathrm{Sp}}(V)/\{\pm 1\}
where
V=V(2n-3)\oplus V(1)
, and
\omega
is a non-degenerate
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
-equivariant symplectic form on
V
, which is unique up to scaling on each factor (for the notation, see §
2.1
). Then
e=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
maps to a subregular nilpotent element
e\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
.

The flag variety
\mathscr{B}
of
G^{\vee}
is identified with:

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}

\displaystyle\ \{0\subsetneq V_{1}\cdots\subsetneq V_{n}\subset V:\dim(V_{i})=i,\text{ and }\omega(V_{n},V_{n})=0\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \{0\subsetneq V_{1}\cdots\subsetneq V_{2n-1}\subsetneq V:V_{i}^{\perp}=V_{2n-i}\},

where
V_{i}^{\perp}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{v\in V:\omega(v,V_{i})=0\}
is the complement with respect to
\omega
. For convenience, also let
V_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}0
and
V_{2n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}V
. The Springer fiber
\mathscr{B}_{e}
is:

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}_{e}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}

\displaystyle\ \{(V_{i})\in\mathscr{B}:eV_{i}\subset V_{i-1}\text{ for }i=1,\dots,n\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \{(V_{i})\in\mathscr{B}:eV_{i}\subset V_{i-1}\text{ for }i=1,\dots,2n\}.

Moreover, for each integer
j\leqslant n
, let
{\mathscr{P}}_{j}
be the partial flag variety of type
j
, i.e.,

{\mathscr{P}}_{j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{0\subsetneq V_{i}\subsetneq V\text{ for }i\neq j,2n-j:V_{i}^{\perp}=V_{2n-i},\dim(V_{i})=i,\text{ and for all }i_{1}<i_{2},V_{i_{1}}\subsetneq V_{i_{2}}\}.

The natural projection
{\mathscr{B}}\rightarrow{\mathscr{P}}_{j}
simply forgets
V_{j}
and
V_{2n-j}
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
e\in\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}
be subregular nilpotent. Then
\mathscr{B}_{e}
is a union of
n+1
copies of
\mathbf{P}^{1}
, and the dual graph of the components is:

\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{1}}
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{2}}^{1}
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{n-2}}
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{n-1}}
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\beta+}
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\beta-}

Proof.

The
n+1
copies of
\mathbf{P}^{1}
are given explicitly as follows:

•

For each
j<n
, consider the partial flag:

V_{i}^{(j)}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}\bigoplus_{k=1}^{i}V(2n-3)_{2n-2k-1}&1\leqslant i<j\\
V(1)_{1}\oplus\bigoplus_{k=0}^{i-1}V(2n-3)_{2n-2k-1}&j<i\leqslant n.\end{cases}

Then let
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}}^{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\pi_{j}^{-1}((V_{i}^{(j)})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n,i\neq j})
.

•

For
j=n
, there are nonzero
v\in V(1)_{1}
and
w\in V(2n-3)_{1}
and for
1\leqslant i<n
, we may let

V^{\pm}_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}\bigoplus_{k=1}^{i}V(2n-3)_{2n-2k-1}&i<n-1\\
{\mathbb{C}}(v\pm w)\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n-2}V(2n-3)_{2n-2k-1}&i=n-1.\end{cases}

Then let
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\beta\pm}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\pi_{n}^{-1}((V_{i}^{\pm})_{1\leqslant i<n})
.

Now,
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
is the union
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}}^{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{n-1}}^{1}\cup\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\beta+}\cup\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\beta-}
.
∎

Note that
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}/2
is generated by
\sigma\in{\mathrm{Sp}}(V)
which acts as
-1
on
V(1)
and as
1
on
V(2n-3)
.

Lemma 4.7
.

The fixed points of the
\sigma
-action on
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
are the points
x_{j}=(W_{i}^{(j)})_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}
for
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
corresponding to the flag

W_{i}^{(j)}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}\bigoplus_{k=1}^{i}V(2n-3)_{2n-2k-1}&i<j\\
V(1)_{1}\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{i-1}V(2n-3)_{2n-2k-1}&i\geqslant j.\end{cases}

When
1<j<n
, we have
x_{j}=\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{j-1}}\cap\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{j}}
.

Now, we may explicitly describe the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-action:

•

for
1\leqslant j<n
, there is an isomorphism
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{j}}\simeq\mathbf{P}(V(1)_{1}\oplus V(2n-3)_{2n-2j-1})
, so
\sigma
acts by an order
2
involution, fixing
x_{j-1}
and
x_{j}
.

•

there are isomorphisms
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\beta\pm}\simeq\mathbf{P}({\mathbb{C}}(v\pm w)\oplus V(2n-3)_{-1})
, and
\sigma
swaps the two copies.

Moreover, for convenience, let
y_{\pm}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{n-1}}\cap\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\beta\pm}
, so
y\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{y_{+},y_{-}\}
is a
\sigma
-invariant divisor of
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
.

We have the following explicit description of the canonical basis.

Lemma 4.8
.

When
G
is of type
\text{B}_{n}
where
n\geqslant 3
, the canonical basis of
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}({\mathscr{B}}_{e}))
consists of:

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}},\operatorname{sgn}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}},\text{ and }{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}^{1}}(-x_{i})[1],{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}^{1}}(-x_{i+1})[1]\text{ for }1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1,\text{ and }{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(-y)[1].

Proof of Lemma
4.4
when
G
is of type B.

The sheaves
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}
and
\operatorname{sgn}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}
clearly have
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)
-equivariance. The
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)
-equivariance is also clear for
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}^{1}}(-x_{i})[1]
for
2\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
, for
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}^{1}}(-x_{i+1})[1]
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-2
, and for
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(-y)[1]
since the divisors
x_{i}
for
1<i<n
and
y
are all
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)
-equivariant (as they are realized as intersections of components of
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
).

The only objects left are
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{1}}}\!(-x_{1})[1]
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{n-1}}}\!(-x_{n})[1]
. But they have a
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)
-equivariance arising from the isomorphisms:

{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{1}}}\!(-x_{1})[1]\simeq\operatorname{sgn}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{1}}}\!(-x_{2})[1],\hskip 8.5359pt{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{n-1}}}\!(-x_{n})[1]\simeq\operatorname{sgn}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{n-1}}}\!(-x_{n-1})[1].\qed

Thus, we have:

Proposition 4.9
.

When
G
is of type
\text{B}_{n}
where
n\geqslant 3
, the irreducible exotic coherent sheaves of
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U}))
consists of:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}},\ {\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{e}}[-1],\ \operatorname{sgn}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{e}}[-1],\ \operatorname{Ind}_{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}^{G^{\vee}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}^{1}}(-x_{i}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ind}_{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}^{G^{\vee}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}^{1}}(-x_{i+1})\text{ for }1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1,\text{ and }\operatorname{Ind}_{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}^{G^{\vee}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(-y).

4.3.
When
G
is of type C

Let
G^{\vee}={\mathrm{SO}}(V)
where
V
is a
2n+1
-dimensional vector space with a non-degenerate symmetric form
(-,-)
. Then the flag variety
\mathscr{B}
is identified with:

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}

\displaystyle\ \{0\subsetneq V_{1}\cdots\subsetneq V_{n}\subset V:\dim(V_{i})=i,\text{ and }(V_{n},V_{n})=0\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \{0\subsetneq V_{1}\cdots\subsetneq V_{2n}\subsetneq V:V_{i}^{\perp}=V_{2n+1-i}\}.

For convenience, also let
V_{0}=0
and
V_{2n}=V
. Given a nilpotent element
e\in\mathfrak{so}(V)
, the Springer fiber
\mathscr{B}_{e}
is:

\mathscr{B}_{e}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{(V_{i})\in\mathscr{B}:eV_{i}\subset V_{i-1}\text{ for }i=1,\dots,n\}=\{(V_{i})\in\mathscr{B}:eV_{i}\subset V_{i-1}\text{ for }i=1,\dots,2n+1\}.

Here, the two definitions agree, since
(ev,w)+(v,ew)=0
for all
v,w\in V
implies
eV_{i}\subset V_{i-1}
and
eV_{i-1}^{\perp}\subset V_{i}^{\perp}
are equivalent.

Example 4.10
.

The subregular nilpotent orbit corresponds to the partition
[2n-1,1^{2}]
, and is realized on
V=V(2n-2)\oplus V(0)^{\oplus 2}
, with the symmetric form as above. The centralizer of the
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
-triple is
{\mathrm{O}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
,
2
2

2

Here
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
acts on
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
by
z\mapstochar\rightarrow z^{-1}
.
which acts tautologically on
V(0)^{\oplus 2}
, and via the determinant on
V(2n-2)
. Explicitly, let
V(0)^{\oplus 2}={\mathbb{C}}e_{+}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}e_{-}
, with the invariant form
(e_{+},e_{+})=(e_{-},e_{-})=0
and
(e_{+},e_{-})=1
. Then
(z,\pm 1)\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
acts as
e_{+}\mapstochar\rightarrow z^{\pm 1}e_{\pm}
and
e_{-}\mapstochar\rightarrow z^{\mp 1}e_{\mp}
.

Moreover, for each integer
j\leqslant n
, let
{\mathscr{P}}_{j}
be the partial flag variety of type
j
,
3
3

3

In the notation of §
2.1
,
{\mathscr{P}}_{j}={\mathscr{P}}_{\alpha_{j}}
for
j<n
and
{\mathscr{P}}_{n}={\mathscr{P}}_{\beta}
.
i.e.,

{\mathscr{P}}_{j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{0\subsetneq V_{i}\subsetneq V\text{ for }i\neq j,2n+1-j:V_{i}^{\perp}=V_{2n+1-i},\dim(V_{i})=i,\text{ and for all }i_{1}<i_{2},V_{i_{1}}\subsetneq V_{i_{2}}\}.

The natural projection
{\mathscr{B}}\rightarrow{\mathscr{P}}_{j}
simply forgets
V_{j}
and
V_{2n+1-j}
.
4
4

4

One should
a priori
expect
{\mathscr{P}}_{n}
to behave differently than
{\mathscr{P}}_{j}
for
j<n
, since the two forgotten vector spaces are consecutive.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
e\in\mathfrak{so}_{2n+1}
be subregular nilpotent. Then
\mathscr{B}_{e}
is a union of
2n-1
copies of
\mathbf{P}^{1}
.

Proof.

Identify
V
with
{\mathbb{C}}e_{+}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}e_{-}\oplus V(2n-2)
, where
(e_{+},e_{+})=(e_{-},e_{-})=0
and
(e_{+},e_{-})=1
. Then the image of
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}
in
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
is subregular nilpotent. The
2n-1
copies of
\mathbf{P}^{1}
are given as follows:

•

For each
j<n
and a sign
\pm
, consider the partial flags:

V_{i}^{(j,\pm)}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}\bigoplus_{k=1}^{i}V(2n-2)_{2n-2k}&1\leqslant i<j\\
{\mathbb{C}}e_{\pm}\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{i-1}V(2n-2)_{2n-2k}&j<i\leqslant n.\end{cases}

Then we let
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}\pm}^{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\pi_{j}^{-1}((V_{i}^{(j,\pm)})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n,i\neq j})
.

•

For
j=n
, for
i<n
, let

V_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigoplus_{k=1}^{i}V(2n-2)_{2n-2k}.

Then let
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\beta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\pi_{n}^{-1}((V_{i})_{1\leqslant i<n})
. Here
\mathbf{P}_{\beta}^{1}
is identified with the flag variety of
{\mathrm{SO}}_{3}\simeq{\mathrm{PGL}}_{2}
, which is isomorphic to
\mathbf{P}^{1}
.

Now,
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
is the union
\bigcup_{j=1}^{n-1}(\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}+}^{1}\cup\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}-}^{1})\cup\mathbf{P}_{\beta}^{1}
.
∎

Remark 4.12
.

In the notation of §
2.1
, we have
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}}=\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{j}+}\cup\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{j}-}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\beta}=\mathbf{P}_{\beta}^{1}
.

As in Example
4.10
, note that
(z,\pm 1)\in{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}\simeq{\mathrm{O}}_{2}={\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
acts on
V(2n-2)
as
\pm 1
, on
e_{+}
as
e_{+}\mapstochar\rightarrow z^{\pm 1}e_{\pm}
, and on
e_{-}
as
e_{-}\mapstochar\rightarrow z^{\mp 1}e_{\mp}
. Thus explicitly,
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
acts on
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
by swapping
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{j}+}
and
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{j}-}
, and acts as an involution on
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\beta}
. The
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-action is also expressed explicitly:

Lemma 4.13
.

The
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-action on
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
acts with weight one on all copies of
\mathbf{P}^{1}
in
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
, with fixed points
x_{j}^{\pm}
(where
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
) corresponding to the following flag:

W_{i}^{(j,\pm)}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}\bigoplus_{k=1}^{i}V(2n-2)_{2n-2k}&i<j\\
{\mathbb{C}}e_{\pm}\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{i-1}V(2n-2)_{2n-2k}&j\leqslant i\leqslant n.\end{cases}

The restriction of the
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-action on
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}}
is attracting at
x_{j}^{-}
and
x_{j+1}^{+}
, and repelling at
x_{j}^{+}
and
x_{j+1}^{-}
, in the sense of
[
Ach21
, §2.10]
, and the restriction of the
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-action on
\mathbf{P}_{\beta}
is attracting at
x_{n}^{-}
and repelling at
x_{n}^{+}
.

For convenience, we define:

Definition 4.14
.

Let
x_{j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{x_{j}^{+},x_{j}^{-}\}
, which a
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
-equivariant divisor in
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}}=\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}+}^{1}\cup\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{j}-}
.

The
{\mathrm{O}}_{2}
-action on
\mathbf{P}_{\beta}
is isomorphic to
(a,\pm 1)\in{\mathrm{O}}_{2}=\mathbb{G}_{m}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
acting on
\mathbf{P}^{1}
via the Möbius transformation
z\mapstochar\rightarrow az^{\pm 1}
.
Let
\mathrm{Pin}_{2}=\mathbb{G}_{m}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2\rightarrow{\mathrm{O}}_{2}
be the double cover, defined by
(z,\pm 1)\mapstochar\rightarrow(z^{2},\pm 1)
. It lies in a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Pin}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{O}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{PGL}}_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2:1}

For
k>0
write
\xi_{k/2}
for the two-dimensional representation of
\mathrm{Pin}_{2}
obtained by inducing the representation
{\mathbb{C}}\langle k\rangle
of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
to
\mathrm{Pin}_{2}
. Note that the representation
\xi_{k/2}
of
\mathrm{Pin}_{2}
factors through
{\mathrm{O}}_{2}
exactly when
k
is even.

Remark 4.15
.

For any
k\geqslant 0
, the object
\xi_{k+1/2}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(1,0)\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\mathrm{Pin}_{2}}(\mathbf{P}^{1})
lives in the sub-category
\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{O}}_{2}}(\mathbf{P}^{1})
. Indeed,
\ker(\mathrm{Pin}_{2}\rightarrow{\mathrm{O}}_{2})=\{\pm I_{2}\}\subseteq{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
acts as
-1
on both
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(1,0)
and
\xi_{k+1/2}
.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
{\mathrm{O}}_{2}=\mathbb{G}_{m}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
act on
\mathbf{P}^{1}
by
z\mapstochar\rightarrow az^{\pm 1}
. Then for any
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{O}}_{2}}(\mathbf{P}^{1})
whose underlying coherent sheaf is a direct sum of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(-1)
, there are integers
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\geqslant 0
such that

\mathcal{F}\simeq(\xi_{k_{1}+1/2}+\cdots+\xi_{k_{n}+1/2})\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(1,0).

Proof.

By viewing
\mathcal{F}
as an object of
\operatorname{Coh}^{\mathrm{Pin}_{2}}(\mathbf{P}^{1})
, the tensor product
\mathcal{F}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(-1,0)
has underlying coherent sheaf isomorphic to a direct sum of
{\mathcal{O}}
. Thus (by taking coherent cohomology) there is some representation
V
of
\mathrm{Pin}_{2}
such that
\mathcal{F}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(-1,0)\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}\otimes V
. Now,

\mathcal{F}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}}(1,0)\otimes V

living in the subcategory
\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{O}}_{2}}(\mathbf{P}^{1})
is equivalent to asking
-I_{2}\in{\mathrm{O}}_{2}
to act as
-1
on
V
, which means
V
must be a direct sum of
\xi_{k+1/2}
for
k\geqslant 0
.
∎

We have the following description of the canonical basis.

Lemma 4.17
.

When
G
is of type
\text{C}_{n}
where
n\geqslant 2
, the canonical basis of
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}({\mathscr{B}}_{e}))
consists of

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}},\ \operatorname{sgn}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}},\ \xi_{k}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}\text{ for $k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}$},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}(kx_{i}-(k+1)x_{i+1})[1]\text{ for $k\in{\mathbb{Z}}$ and $1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1$, and }\xi_{k+1/2}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(1,0)[1]\text{ for $k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geqslant 0}$}.

Proof of Lemma
4.4
when
G
is of type C.

The
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
-equivariance on
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}
,
\operatorname{sgn}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}
, and
\xi_{k}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}
is clear. Since
x_{i}
for
i>1
are characterized as intersection of components of
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
, they are
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
-equivariant, and hence
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}(kx_{i}-(k+1)x_{i+1})[1]
has
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)
-equivariance for
i>1
.

To define
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)
-equivariant structure on
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}}}(kx_{1}-(k+1)x_{2})[1]
, it suffices to define
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)^{\circ}
-equivariant structure on
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}+}}(kx_{1}^{+}-(k+1)x_{2}^{+})[1]
. But then

{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}+}}(kx_{1}^{+}-(k+1)x_{2}^{+})[1]\simeq{\mathbb{C}}\langle-k\rangle\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}+}}\!(-x_{2}^{+})[1],

where
{\mathbb{C}}\langle-k\rangle
is a representation of
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)^{\circ}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}+}}\!(-x_{2}^{+})
has
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)^{\circ}
-equivariance by the discussion above.

Finally, note that the unipotent radical of
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)
acts on
\mathbf{P}_{\beta}^{1}
with two fixed points, i.e., is given by a homomorphism to
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. But unipotent groups do not admit non-trivial homomorphisms to
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, so the action of
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)
on
\mathbf{P}_{\beta}^{1}
factors through
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
. Thus any
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
-equivariant sheaf on
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
such as
\xi_{k+1/2}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(1,0)[1]
admit a
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)
-equivariant structure.
∎

Thus, we have:

Proposition 4.18
.

When
G
is of type
\text{C}_{n}
where
n\geqslant 2
, the irreducible exotic coherent sheaves of
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U}))
consists of

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}},\ {\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}[-1],\ \operatorname{sgn}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}[-1],\ \xi_{k}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}[-1]\text{ for $k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}$},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ind}_{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}^{G^{\vee}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}(kx_{i}-(k+1)x_{i+1})\text{ for $k\in{\mathbb{Z}}$ and $1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1$,}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ind}_{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}^{G^{\vee}}\big(\xi_{k+1/2}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(1,0)\big)\text{ for $k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geqslant 0}$}.

G

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
G
be of type F
4
, and let
e\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
be subregular nilpotent. Then
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
acts on
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
as an involution on
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{2}}
, and swaps the two components of
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{3}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{4}}
.

Proof.

By
[
Slo80
]
, using notation from §
2.7
, the resolution
\pi_{S}\colon\widetilde{S}\rightarrow S
is isomorphic to the resolution of the type E
6
Kleinian singularity
X^{4}+Y^{3}+Z^{2}=0
. The group
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
acts as
(X,Y,Z)\mapstochar\rightarrow(-X,Y,-Z)
on
S
, which acts as a graph automorphism of the Dynkin diagram of E
6
, as in Example
2.18
. Thus, everything is clear except that the
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
-action on
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}}
is nontrivial.

However, since
Z_{\mathrm{num}}
(defined in §
2.7
) is the highest weight of the E
6
lattice, and
Z_{\mathrm{num}}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}}<0
, i.e.
\alpha_{1}^{\vee}
is connected to the affine vertex in the E
6
Dynkin diagram, the line
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}}
appears in the blow-up
\widetilde{X}
at a point of
X:x^{4}+y^{3}+z^{2}=0
. Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{3}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{((X,Y,Z),(S:T:U))\in\mathbf{A}^{3}\times\mathbf{P}^{2}:XT=YS,XU=ZS,YU=ZT\}
, which has an affine open
\mathbf{A}^{3}\simeq\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{3}_{S\neq 0}:(X,Y_{0},Z_{0})\mapstochar\rightarrow(X,XY_{0},XZ_{0})
, the intersection
\widetilde{X}\cap\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{3}_{S\neq 0}
is identitified with
\{(X,Y_{0},Z_{0})\in\mathbf{A}^{3}:X^{2}+XY_{0}^{3}+Z_{0}^{2}=0\}
. Moreover, the involution
(X,Y,Z)\mapstochar\rightarrow(-X,Y,-Z)
induces the involution
(X,Y_{0},Z_{0})\mapstochar\rightarrow(-X,-Y_{0},Z_{0})
. The pre-image of the exceptional divisor is then
\{(0,Y_{0},Z_{0})\in\mathbf{A}^{3}:Z_{0}^{2}=0\}
, which is a (double) line, on which the involution acts non-trivially.
∎

We introduce the following notation, for convenience, when
G
is of type F
4
:

•

x_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}\cap\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i+1}}
for
i=1,2,3
. Here
x_{1}
is a point, while
x_{2}
and
x_{3}
are two points.

•

For
i=1,2
, let
y_{i}
be other fixed point of the involution on
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}
.
5
5

5

y_{i}
is uniquely determined, since all involutions of
\mathbf{P}^{1}
have two fixed points.

.

when

The same proof as in type B works.

We have the following explicit description of the canonical basis:

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
G
be of type G
2
, and let

{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
acts on
{\mathscr{B}}_{e}
as an involution on
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}
and permutes the three components of
\mathbf{P}_{\beta}
.

Proof.

By
[
Slo80
]
, using notation from §
2.7
, the resolution
\pi_{S}\colon\widetilde{S}\rightarrow S
is isomorphic to the resolution of the type D
4
Kleinian singularity
x^{3}+y^{3}+z^{2}=0
. The group
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
acts as the full automorphism group of
\pi_{S}
, which acts as a graph automorphism of the Dynkin diagram of E
6
, as in Example
2.18
. Thus, everything is clear except that the
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
-action on
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}}
is nontrivial.

However, since
Z_{\mathrm{num}}
(defined in §
2.7
) is the highest weight of the D
4
lattice, and
Z_{\mathrm{num}}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}}<0
, i.e.
\alpha^{\vee}
is connected to the affine vertex in the D
4
Dynkin diagram, the line
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}
appears in the blow-up
\widetilde{S}
at
(0,0,0)
of
S:X^{3}+Y^{3}+Z^{2}=0
. Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{3}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{((X,Y,Z),(S:T:U))\in\mathbf{A}^{3}\times\mathbf{P}^{2}:XT=YS,XU=ZS,YU=ZT\}
, which has an affine open
\mathbf{A}^{3}\simeq\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{3}_{S\neq 0}:(X,Y_{0},Z_{0})\mapstochar\rightarrow(X,XY_{0},XZ_{0})
, the intersection
\widetilde{X}\cap\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}^{3}_{S\neq 0}
is identified with
\{(X,Y_{0},Z_{0})\in\mathbf{A}^{3}:X^{2}+XY_{0}^{3}+Z_{0}^{2}=0\}
. Moreover, the involution
(X,Y,Z)\mapstochar\rightarrow(-X,Y,-Z)
induces the involution
(X,Y_{0},Z_{0})\mapstochar\rightarrow(-X,-Y_{0},Z_{0})
. The pre-image of the exceptional divisor is then
\{(0,X_{0},Z_{0})\in\mathbf{A}^{3}:Z_{0}^{2}=0\}
, which is a (double) line, on which the involution acts non-trivially.
∎

For convenience, let
x\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbf{P}_{\beta}
, which consists of three points; one in each component of
\mathbf{P}_{\beta}
.

Lemma 4.23
.

Let
G
be of type G
2
. The canonical basis of
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}({\mathscr{B}}_{e}))
consists of

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}},\ \operatorname{sgn}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}},\ \operatorname{std}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}},\ {\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}\!(-x)[1],\ {\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(-y)[1],

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}}(1,0)[1],\ {\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}}(0,-1)[1],\text{ and }\operatorname{std}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}}(1,0)[1].

Proof of Lemma
4.4
when
G
is of type G
2
.

Clearly
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}
,
\operatorname{sgn}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}
, and
\operatorname{std}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}
have
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)
-equivariance. Moreover
x=\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbf{P}_{\beta}
is
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)
-equivariant, so
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}\!(-x)[1]
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(-y)[1]\simeq\operatorname{sgn}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}\!(-x)[1]
have
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)
-equivariance.

Moreover, the unipotent radical of
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)
acts on
\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}\simeq\mathbf{P}^{1}
with three fixed points, i.e., is trivial. Thus
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}}(1,0)[1]
,
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}}(0,-1)[1]
, and
\operatorname{std}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}}(1,0)[1]
are
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}
-equivariant sheaves, and hence automatically also
{\mathrm{Z}}(e)
-equivariant sheaves.
∎

Thus, we have:

Proposition 4.24
.

Let
G
be of type G
2
. The irreducible exotic coherent sheaves of
D^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U}))
consists of

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}},\ {\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}[-1],\ \operatorname{sgn}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}[-1],\ \operatorname{std}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}}}[-1],\ \operatorname{Ind}_{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}^{G^{\vee}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}\!(-x),\ \operatorname{Ind}_{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}^{G^{\vee}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(-y),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ind}_{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}^{G^{\vee}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}}(1,0),\ \operatorname{Ind}_{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}^{G^{\vee}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}}(0,-1),\ \operatorname{Ind}_{{\mathrm{Z}}(e)}^{G^{\vee}}\big(\operatorname{std}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha}}(1,0)\big).

5.
Proof of Proposition
1.10

First, we make some preliminary observations:

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
G
be arbitrary. Then
C_{1}=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}]
and
C_{s_{0}}=-[{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}]
. Moreover, the basis elements satisfy a twist by sign of (
8
):

\displaystyle T_{t}C_{1}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-C_{1}-C_{s_{0}}&t=s_{0}\\
-C_{1}&t\neq s_{0}.\end{cases}

Proof.

The fact that
C_{1}=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}]
is general (since
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}}
is in the heart of the exotic
t
-structure of
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{Coh}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}))
). Moreover, by Remark
2.26
,
C_{s_{0}}=-[{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}]
. Any
t\in\widehat{S}\backslash\{s_{0}\}=S
lies in
W
, so acts on
[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}]
by
\operatorname{sgn}(t)=-1
.
∎

We proceed by a case-work on the type of
G
.

5.1.
When
G
is simply-laced

In this case,
[
BKK24
]
provides an explicit description of the
\widehat{W}
-module
K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U})
:

Lemma 5.2

(
[
BKK24
, Prop 5.16]
)
.

Let
G
be of type D or E, of rank
n
. There is an isomorphism of
\widehat{W}
-modules

K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U})\simeq\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{sgn}}

given by

C_{1}=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}]\mapstochar\rightarrow d\otimes 1,C_{w_{0}}=[{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}]\mapstochar\rightarrow\alpha_{0}^{\vee}\otimes 1,C_{w_{i}}=-[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}(-1)]\mapstochar\rightarrow\alpha_{i}^{\vee},

where
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}K\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}d
, which is a
\widehat{W}
-module by the action
s_{i}(x)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}x-\langle\alpha_{i},x\rangle\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
, for
x\in\mathfrak{h}
and
i=0,1,\dots,n
.

Lemma 5.3

(
[
BKK24
, Prop 6.17]
)
.

Let
G
be of type A, of rank
n
. There is an isomorphism of
\widehat{W}
-modules

K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U})\simeq\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{\infty,{\mathbb{Z}}}\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{\operatorname{sgn}}

given by

C_{\nu_{i}}\mapstochar\rightarrow-\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\otimes 1
and
{C}_{0}\mapstochar\rightarrow d\otimes 1,

where
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{\infty,{\mathbb{Z}}}
is defined in
[
BKK24
, §6.4.2]
.

Now, Proposition
1.10
reduces to the following statements about representations of
\widehat{W}
:

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
G
be of type D or E. Then there is an isomorphism

\widetilde{E}_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}\simeq\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathbb{Z}}

sending
e_{w_{i}}
to
-\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
for
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
and sending
e_{1}
to
d
where
w_{i}\in c_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}
is defined in
[
BKK24
, Cor 5.2]
.

Proof.

It suffices to check (
7
) and (
8
). Indeed, we have:

\displaystyle s_{j}(d)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}d-\alpha_{0}^{\vee}&j=0\\
d&j\neq 0.\end{cases}

\displaystyle s_{j}(-\alpha_{i}^{\vee})=\langle\alpha_{i}^{\vee},\alpha_{j}\rangle\alpha_{j}^{\vee}-\alpha_{i}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}&i=j\\
-\alpha_{i}^{\vee}&\text{$i$ and $j$ are not adjacent in the Dynkin graph}\\
-\alpha_{i}^{\vee}-\alpha_{j}^{\vee}&\text{$i$ and $j$ are adjacent in the Dynkin graph}.\end{cases}

∎

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
G
be of type A. Then there is an isomorphism

\widetilde{E}_{\mathrm{subreg}}^{0}\simeq\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{\infty,{\mathbb{Z}}}

sending
e_{s_{i}\cdots s_{0}}
to
\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
for
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
, and sending
e_{1}
to
d
.

Proof.

Again, it suffices to check (
7
) and (
8
):

\displaystyle s_{j}(d)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}d-\alpha_{0}^{\vee}&j=0\\
d&j\neq 0,\end{cases}

\displaystyle s_{j}(\alpha_{i}^{\vee})=s_{j}(\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{i+1})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\epsilon_{i+1}-\epsilon_{i}&j=i\\
\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{i+2}=\alpha_{i}^{\vee}+\alpha_{i+1}^{\vee}&j=i+1\\
\epsilon_{i-1}-\epsilon_{i+1}=\alpha_{i}^{\vee}+\alpha_{i-1}^{\vee}&j=i-1\\
\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{i+1}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

∎

5.2.
When
G
is of type B

Let
G
be of type
\text{B}_{n}
where
n\geqslant 3
. The canonical basis of
K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U})
was described in Lemma

, so our first task is to compute which element of
c^{0}_{\mathrm{subreg}}
, described in Lemma

corresponds to each basis element of
K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{U})
(see Proposition
5.10
). First, we compute some equivariant line bundles:

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
G
be of type
\text{B}_{n}
where
n\geqslant 3
, and let
e\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
be subregular nilpotent. For
\gamma=a_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}\epsilon_{n}\in Q^{\vee}
there are isomorphisms of
{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-equivariant line bundles:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}^{1}}(a_{i}x_{i}-a_{i+1}x_{i+1})\text{ for $1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1$}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(a_{n}y).

Proof.

For
i=1,\dots,n-1
, the underlying line bundle of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}(\gamma)
has fiber over
V_{i-1}\subsetneq V\subsetneq V_{i+1}
isomorphic to
(V/V_{i-1})^{\otimes-a_{i}}\otimes(V_{i+1}/V)^{\otimes-a_{i+1}}
, i.e., it is
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{i}}}\!(a_{i}-a_{i+1})
.
6
6

6

Alternatively, use Lemma
2.5
.
Moreover, the underlying line bundle of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(\gamma)
has fiber over
V_{n-1}\subsetneq V\subsetneq V_{n+1}
isomorphic to
(V/V_{n-1})^{\otimes-a_{n}}
, i.e., it is
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}\!(a_{n})
.

Moreover, the fiber of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{B}}(\gamma)
over
x_{j}
(as in Lemma
4.7
) is

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{j-1}V(2n-3)_{2n-2i-1}^{\otimes-a_{i}}\otimes V(1)_{1}^{\otimes-a_{j}}\otimes\bigotimes_{i=j+1}^{n}V(2n-3)_{2n-2i+1}^{\otimes-a_{i}},

so
\sigma\neq 1\in{\mathrm{Z}}_{G}(e)
acts as
(-1)^{a_{j}}
on the fiber of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{B}}(\gamma)
over
x_{j}
.
∎

As an immediate corollary, we obtain an explicit description of the
Q^{\vee}
-action on
K^{{\mathrm{Z}}_{e}}({\mathscr{B}}_{e})
in type B:

Corollary 5.7
.

\gamma=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\epsilon_{i}\in Q^{\vee}
acts on
K^{G^{\vee}}\!(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathrm{subreg}})\simeq K^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}({\mathscr{B}}_{e})
as:

\displaystyle t_{\gamma}\cdot[{\mathbb{C}}_{x_{1}}]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{sgn}^{a_{1}}\cdot[{\mathbb{C}}_{x_{1}}]

\displaystyle t_{\gamma}\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{i}}}]

\displaystyle=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{i}}}\!(-x_{i})]+(1+\cdots+\operatorname{sgn}^{a_{i}})[{\mathbb{C}}_{x_{i}}]-(1+\cdots+\operatorname{sgn}^{a_{i+1}-1})[{\mathbb{C}}_{x_{i+1}}]

\displaystyle t_{\gamma}\cdot[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}]

\displaystyle=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(-y)]-(a_{n}-1)[{\mathbb{C}}_{y}].

Proof.

Follows from induction on the standard exact sequence

0\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{j}}}\!\big((a_{j}-1)x_{j}-a_{j+1}x_{j+1}\big)\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\alpha_{j}}}\!(a_{j}x_{j}-a_{j+1}x_{j+1})\rightarrow\operatorname{sgn}^{a_{j}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{x_{j}}\rightarrow 0.\qed

These canonical basis elements, described in Lemma
4.9
, must correspond to elements of the subregular two-sided cell of
\widehat{W}
, as enumerated in Lemma
3.6
. To match the elements, recall that for
\gamma\in Q^{\vee}
there is an equality
[
BKK24
, §6.7]

t_{\gamma}\cdot 1=\sum_{w_{\nu}\leqslant w_{\gamma}}\mathbf{m}_{w_{\nu}}^{w_{\gamma}}C_{\nu}.

We calculate the expression for all of the
\gamma\in Q^{\vee}
such that

.
Using Lemma

and Lemma

, in case (

,

(33)

[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}(\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{i+1})]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}(\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2})]+\sum_{j=2}^{i}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}}}\!(-x_{j+1})]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}]+\sum_{j=2}^{i}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}}}\!(-x_{j+1})],

so

C_{s_{i}s_{i-1}\cdots s_{0}}=C_{w_{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{i+1}}}=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}\!(-x_{i+1})].

In case (

.

Moreover, (

\displaystyle[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}(\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{i})]

(35)

\displaystyle=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}]+\sum_{j=2}^{n-1}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}}}\!(-x_{j+1})]+[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(-y)]+\sum_{j=i}^{n-1}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}}}\!(-x_{j})],

Note that the above calculation actually gives a change-of-basis formula. For convenience, let

(36)

d_{j}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }j=1\\
2&\text{if }2\leqslant j\leqslant n-1.\end{cases}

Lemma 5.8
.

For any
2\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
,

(37)

\displaystyle[{\mathbb{C}}_{x_{1}}]

\displaystyle=[{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}}}\!(-x_{1})]-\sum_{j=2}^{n-1}(1+\operatorname{sgn})[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}}}\!(-x_{j})]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(-y)]

(38)

\displaystyle[{\mathbb{C}}_{x_{i}}]

\displaystyle=[{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}]-\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}d_{j}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}}}\!(-x_{j+1})]-\sum_{j=i}^{n-1}(1+\operatorname{sgn})[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}}}\!(-x_{j})]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(-y)]

(39)

\displaystyle[{\mathbb{C}}_{y}]

\displaystyle=(1+\operatorname{sgn})[{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}]-(1+\operatorname{sgn})\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}d_{j}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}}}\!(-x_{j})]-2[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(-y)].

Proof.

When

, (

[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}}}]=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}]+\sum_{j=2}^{n-1}\big([{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}}}\!(-x_{j+1})]+[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{j}}}\!(-x_{j})]\big)+[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(-y)],

which, since

, gives (

). Now the rest follows from observing that from the exact sequences

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}\!(-x_{i})\rightarrow

\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{x_{i}}\rightarrow 0

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}\!(-x_{i+1})\rightarrow

\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{x_{i+1}}\rightarrow 0

which gives
[{\mathbb{C}}_{x_{i+1}}]=[{\mathbb{C}}_{x_{i}}]+[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}\!(-x_{i})]-[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}\!(-x_{i+1})]
.
∎

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
G
be of type
\text{B}_{n}
where

. The canonical basis of

are:

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{U}}]

\displaystyle C_{s_{0}}

\displaystyle=-[{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}]

\displaystyle C_{s_{1}s_{2}s_{0}}

\displaystyle=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{1}}}\!(-x_{2})]

\displaystyle C_{s_{i}\cdots s_{0}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}\!(-x_{i+1})]&\text{if }2\leqslant i\leqslant n-1\\
[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\beta}}(-y)]&\text{if }i=n\end{cases}

\displaystyle C_{s_{i}\cdots s_{n-1}s_{n}s_{n-1}\cdots s_{2}s_{0}}

\displaystyle=[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{P}_{\alpha_{i}}}\!(-x_{i})]

\displaystyle C_{s_{0}s_{2}\cdots s_{n-1}s_{n}s_{n-1}\cdots s_{2}s_{0}}

\displaystyle=-[\operatorname{sgn}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathscr{B}}_{e}}].

Proof of Proposition
1.10
when
G
is of type B.

It suffices to check the basis in Proposition

satisfies relations (

) (note that (

First, note that for each simple coroot
\alpha^{\vee}
of
\mathfrak{g}
and

a canonical basis element

is supported on

. Thus for
\alpha^{\prime}\in\Delta
with

(equivalently,

and

are disjoint),

as follows, e.g., from the description of the Hecke algebra action in

Lus02
]
.

Now (
33
) gives, for
i\geqslant 2
,

(42)

(-1)^{i}s_{i}\cdots s_{2}s_{0}C_{1}=C_{1}+C_{s_{0}}+\sum_{j=2}^{i}C_{s_{j}\cdots s_{2}s_{0}}.

When
i=2
the above is
s_{2}s_{0}C_{1}=C_{1}+C_{s_{0}}+C_{s_{2}s_{0}}
, which gives

\displaystyle s_{2}C_{s_{0}}

\displaystyle=-s_{2}s_{0}C_{1}-s_{2}C_{1}

(43)

\displaystyle=-C_{1}-C_{s_{0}}-C_{s_{2}s_{0}}+C_{1}

\displaystyle=-C_{s_{0}}-C_{s_{2}s_{0}}.

For
i>2
we have

(44)

s_{i}C_{s_{0}}=s_{i}(-C_{1}-s_{1}C_{1})=C_{1}+s_{1}C_{1}=-C_{s_{0}},

and

(45)

s_{0}C_{s_{0}}=s_{0}(-C_{1}-s_{0}C_{1})=-s_{0}C_{1}-C_{1}=C_{s_{0}}.

Equations (
43
), (
44
), and (
45
) which together is exactly (
7
) twisted by sign for
C_{s_{0}}
.

When
i\leqslant n-2
by applying
-s_{i+1}
on both sides of (
42
),

C_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{i+1}C_{s_{j}\cdots s_{2}s_{0}}=C_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{i-1}C_{s_{j}\cdots s_{0}}-s_{i+1}C_{s_{i}\cdots s_{2}s_{0}},

i.e.,

(46)

s_{i+1}C_{s_{i}\cdots s_{2}s_{0}}=-C_{s_{i}\cdots s_{0}}-C_{s_{i+1}\cdots s_{0}}.

Similarly, applying
-s_{i}
to both sides of (

) gives

\displaystyle C_{1}+C_{s_{0}}+\sum_{j=2}^{i-1}C_{s_{j}\cdots s_{0}}

\displaystyle=C_{1}+C_{s_{0}}+\sum_{j=2}^{i-2}C_{s_{j}\cdots s_{0}}-s_{i}C_{s_{i-1}\cdots s_{0}}-s_{i}C_{s_{i}\cdots s_{0}}

\displaystyle=C_{1}+C_{s_{0}}+\sum_{j=2}^{i}C_{s_{j}\cdots s_{0}}-s_{i}C_{s_{i}\cdots s_{0}}

s_{i}C_{s_{i}\cdots s_{0}}=C_{s_{i}\cdots s_{0}}.

Finally, applying

\displaystyle C_{1}+C_{s_{0}}+\sum_{j=2}^{i}C_{s_{j}\cdots s_{2}s_{0}}

\displaystyle=C_{1}+C_{s_{0}}+\sum_{j=2}^{i-3}C_{s_{j}\cdots s_{2}s_{0}}-s_{i-1}C_{s_{i-2}\cdots s_{0}}-s_{i-1}C_{s_{i-1}\cdots s_{0}}-s_{i-1}C_{s_{i}\cdots s_{0}}

\displaystyle=C_{1}+C_{s_{0}}+\sum_{j=2}^{i-1}C_{s_{j}\cdots s_{2}s_{0}}-C_{s_{i-1}\cdots s_{0}}-s_{i-1}C_{s_{i}\cdots s_{0}},

i.e.,

Equations (

), (
47
), and (

) together is exactly (

) twisted by sign for

.

Similarly, (

) gives, for

so by the same argument as above, we can show (

) for
